# 28. Common Cloud Provider Adapters (AWS, Azure, GCP)

This chapter covers the cloud vendor adapter packages, `MMCA.Common.Aws`, `MMCA.Common.Azure` and `MMCA.Common.Gcp`, plus `MMCA.Common.Azure.Startup`, which holds the three builder-level Azure calls (Key Vault configuration, the Azure Monitor exporter and the DataProtection key ring) with no persistence or messaging dependency. `MMCA.Common.Azure` references it and type-forwards those three classes, so a host that references `MMCA.Common.Azure` still sees them (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/TypeForwards.cs:6-8`). The rule they exist to keep is short: **the core packages reference no cloud SDK**. Every vendor integration lives in an adapter package that references the core, never the reverse, and a host opts in with one registration call per capability ([ADR-134](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/134-cloud-neutral-core-vendor-adapters.html)). So the core defines an abstraction (or an extension point) and ships an inert default. Each adapter type implements that abstraction against one vendor SDK, and all a host has to decide is which package to reference and which calls to make. `[Rubric §3, Clean Architecture]` asks whether dependencies point inward toward policy and away from infrastructure detail. Here the vendor SDKs sit at the outermost ring, and nothing in Domain, Application or Infrastructure names them. `[Rubric §32, Dependency & Supply-Chain]` asks whether a consumer pays only for the dependencies it uses. A host that never calls an AWS adapter never restores an AWS package.

**The abstractions the adapters implement.** Six extension points in the core take an adapter. Their contracts are taught where they are declared, and this chapter only implements them:

- **File storage**: [`IFileStorageService`](group-07-persistence-ef-core.md#ifilestorageservice) (`MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/Interfaces/Infrastructure/Storage/IFileStorageService.cs:15`, [ADR-045](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/045-managed-file-storage-and-avatars.html)). The core default is [`NullFileStorageService`](group-14-module-system-composition.md#nullfilestorageservice) (`MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Storage/NullFileStorageService.cs:14`).
- **Native push**: the pair [`INativePushSender`](group-07-persistence-ef-core.md#inativepushsender) (`MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/Interfaces/Infrastructure/Notifications/INativePushSender.cs:12`) and [`IPushDeviceRegistrar`](group-07-persistence-ef-core.md#ipushdeviceregistrar) (`MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/Interfaces/Infrastructure/Notifications/IPushDeviceRegistrar.cs:12`) ([ADR-044](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/044-native-push-delivery.html)). The core defaults are [`NullNativePushSender`](group-14-module-system-composition.md#nullnativepushsender) and [`NullPushDeviceRegistrar`](group-14-module-system-composition.md#nullpushdeviceregistrar) (`MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Notifications/Push/NullNativePushSender.cs:12`, `NullPushDeviceRegistrar.cs:14`).
- **Broker transport**: [`IBrokerTransport`](group-14-module-system-composition.md#ibrokertransport) (`MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Messaging/Transports/IBrokerTransport.cs:22`), registered through `AddBrokerTransport` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/DependencyInjection.Messaging.cs:174`) and selected by name ([ADR-066](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/066-broker-transport-selection.html), [ADR-118](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/118-message-transport-library-policy.html)). The retry, redelivery and backpressure rules every transport shares live once in [`BrokerTransportPolicies`](group-14-module-system-composition.md#brokertransportpolicies).
- **PostgreSQL connection customization**: [`INpgsqlDataSourceConfigurator`](group-07-persistence-ef-core.md#inpgsqldatasourceconfigurator) (`MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Persistence/DbContexts/INpgsqlDataSourceConfigurator.cs:23`), added through `AddNpgsqlDataSourceConfigurator` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/DependencyInjection.Persistence.cs:24`).
- **Redis connection customization**: [`IRedisConfigurationOptionsConfigurator`](group-09-caching.md#iredisconfigurationoptionsconfigurator) (`MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Caching/IRedisConfigurationOptionsConfigurator.cs:30`), added through `AddRedisConfigurationOptionsConfigurator` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/DependencyInjection.Caching.cs:253`).
- **Host-level ASP.NET Core extension points**: the configuration system (`IConfigurationSource`), the DataProtection key ring (`IXmlRepository`, `IXmlEncryptor`, `IXmlDecryptor`), and the OpenTelemetry pipeline that `AddServiceDefaults()` configures. These are framework types, not MMCA ones, so the adapters plug into them directly.

**Per-vendor membership.** The 119 types fall into one row per capability. A blank cell means the vendor package has no adapter for that capability.

The rows are uneven for real reasons, not because a port is unfinished. GCP ships no broker transport. Its Cloud SQL configurator instead covers the built-in PostgreSQL broker alongside the database contexts (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/GcpServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:72-74`). Azure has two database paths: Entra ID for SQL Server, which arrives through the SqlClient extension the package carries with no registration call (ADR-134, Decision; restated at `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:172-173`), and Entra token login for Azure Database for PostgreSQL through `AddAzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthentication` (`AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:181`). Only Azure ships a Redis configurator (`AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:230-253`). The two broker namespaces are the largest because each transport is framework code on the bare vendor SDK: [`AzureServiceBusTransport`](#azureservicebustransport) runs on Azure.Messaging.ServiceBus alone and [`AmazonSqsTransport`](#amazonsqstransport) on the AWS SDK SQS and SNS clients alone (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Messaging/AzureServiceBusTransport.cs:13-15`, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/Messaging/AmazonSqsTransport.cs:14-16`), with no third-party messaging library between the framework and the broker ([ADR-118](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/118-message-transport-library-policy.html)). The Azure notifications namespace is large for the same reason: the adapter reaches the hub through the framework's own REST client, [`NotificationHubRestClient`](#notificationhubrestclient) behind [`INotificationHubRestClient`](#inotificationhubrestclient), a typed factory `HttpClient` authorized with a Shared Access Signature from the connection string, with its own retry (three retries with exponential backoff, or the hub's `Retry-After`) and a 60-second attempt timeout, so the standard resilience handler is taken off it rather than stacked on top (`AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:84-90`, `:110-132`; `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Notifications/NotificationHubRetryPolicy.cs:26`, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Notifications/NotificationHubRestClient.cs:43`). The GCP notifications namespace keeps its own registry and client interfaces because FCM tokens carry no owner ([`IFcmInstallationRegistry`](#ifcminstallationregistry), [`IFcmMessagingClient`](#ifcmmessagingclient)), which puts Firestore and FirebaseAdmin behind something testable (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/Notifications/FirestoreInstallationRegistry.cs:16`, `FirebaseMessagingClient.cs:17`).

**How the swap works at runtime: inert core defaults, then an adapter override.** `AddInfrastructure` registers every core default with `TryAdd`: `services.TryAddTransient<INativePushSender, NullNativePushSender>()` and its registrar sibling (`MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/DependencyInjection.cs:377-378`), and `services.TryAddTransient<IFileStorageService, NullFileStorageService>()` (`DependencyInjection.cs:385`). The adapters register with a plain `Add`: `services.AddTransient<IFileStorageService, AzureBlobFileStorageService>()` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:71`), `S3FileStorageService` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:68`), `GcsFileStorageService` (`GcpServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:63`), and the push pairs (`AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:133-134`, `AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:194-195`, `GcpServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:166-167`). The adapter therefore wins whichever side runs first. Called before `AddInfrastructure`, it makes the core `TryAdd` skip. Called after, it is the last registration, which is the one a single-service resolve returns. The vendor SDK clients themselves go in with `TryAddSingleton` (for example the `BlobContainerClient` at `AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:64-70`, `S3StorageClientFactory.Create` at `AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:67`, the SNS and DynamoDB clients at `AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:191-192`, and the Firestore and FCM clients at `GcpServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:163-165`). A host that registered its own client first, an emulator endpoint for example, keeps it. `[Rubric §2, Design Patterns]` asks whether patterns solve a real problem. This is the Adapter pattern with a Null Object default: application code depends on one interface, and swapping the cloud is a registration change.

**How a host selects a vendor: reference, call, then configuration as the gate.** Every adapter call is unconditional in the host and a no-op until its configuration is complete. The class summaries say so in identical words (`AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:20-26`, `AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:20-25`, `GcpServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:17-22`), and the bodies enforce it with early returns. Azure Blob needs a `ContainerName` plus an absolute `ServiceUri` or a `ConnectionString` (`AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:52-62`). S3 needs a `Bucket` (`AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:59-62`). GCS needs a `GcsBucket` (`GcpServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:57-60`). Push needs `NativePush:Enabled` plus the vendor's sub-section (`AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:103-108`, `AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:180-185`, `GcpServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:155-160`). The three storage adapters read the same `FileStorage` section, each through its own gate key, so a section that carries only the S3 `Bucket` or the Azure `ContainerName` leaves the GCS call inert (`GcpServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:33-41`). The four token-login calls are gated on a boolean `Enabled` each: `Rds:IamAuthentication`, `CloudSql:IamAuthentication`, `AzurePostgreSql:EntraAuthentication` and `AzureRedis:EntraAuthentication` (`AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:112-116`, `GcpServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:95-99`, `AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:185-191`, `:234-240`). The builder-level calls follow the same posture with a single gate key each: `KeyVault:Uri` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure.Startup/AzureKeyVaultConfigurationExtensions.cs:82`), `SecretsManager:NamePrefix` (`AwsSecretsManagerConfigurationExtensions.cs:82`), `DataProtection:BlobStorageUri`, `S3ObjectUri` or `GcsObjectUri` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure.Startup/AzureDataProtectionExtensions.cs:56`, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/AwsDataProtectionExtensions.cs:23`, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/GoogleCloudDataProtectionExtensions.cs:24`), `APPLICATIONINSIGHTS_CONNECTION_STRING` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure.Startup/AzureMonitorExporterExtensions.cs:18`), `AwsTelemetry:Region` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:105`) and `GoogleCloudTelemetry:ProjectId` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/GoogleCloudTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:23`). The result is that local development and tests take no cloud dependency at startup, and a deployment switches a capability on through configuration alone. The secrets sources are deliberately not called from `AddServiceDefaults()` for exactly that reason (`AzureKeyVaultConfigurationExtensions.cs:59`). They also use one naming convention (a double dash maps to the configuration separator, so `ConnectionStrings--Default` arrives as `ConnectionStrings:Default`), which lets a secret set move between Key Vault and Secrets Manager by renaming its prefix alone. Both points are recorded on `AzureKeyVaultConfigurationExtensions.cs:80` and `AwsSecretsManagerConfigurationExtensions.cs:82`.

**Broker transports are selected by name, not by type.** `AddBrokerMessaging` picks the bus from the string `MessageBus:Provider`. `RabbitMq`, `PostgreSql` and `InProcess` are built in, and any other name must match an [`IBrokerTransport`](group-14-module-system-composition.md#ibrokertransport) an adapter registered earlier. A name with no registered transport throws, listing the known names and naming the call to add (`MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/DependencyInjection.Messaging.cs:26-37`, the throw at `:225-230`). The two adapter calls are therefore one-liners that hand a transport instance to `AddBrokerTransport`: `AddAzureServiceBusTransport()` (`AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:154-155`) and `AddAmazonSqsTransport()` (`AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:86-87`). Each transport reports its provider name, `AzureServiceBus` or `AmazonSqs`, through `Name` (`AzureServiceBusTransport.cs:40`, `:59`; `AmazonSqsTransport.cs:43`, `:62`). The ordering is load-bearing: the adapter call must come BEFORE `AddBrokerMessaging`, which picks the transport while it registers the bus (`AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:140-142`, `AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:74-76`). Inside, the two transports have the same shape: a connection ([`AzureServiceBusConnection`](#azureservicebusconnection), [`AmazonSqsConnection`](#amazonsqsconnection)), entity naming ([`AzureServiceBusEntityNames`](#azureservicebusentitynames), [`AmazonSqsEntityNames`](#amazonsqsentitynames)), a topology provisioner ([`AzureServiceBusTopologyProvisioner`](#azureservicebustopologyprovisioner), [`AmazonSqsTopologyProvisioner`](#amazonsqstopologyprovisioner)), a publisher, a receiver, a delivery handler, a header mapping and a settlement interface ([`IAzureServiceBusSettlement`](#iazureservicebussettlement), [`IAmazonSqsSettlement`](#iamazonsqssettlement)). With `MessageBus:ProvisionTopology` on (the default) the receiver creates the topics, the host's queue and its subscriptions idempotently when it starts, and the publisher creates a missing topic on its first publish (`AzureServiceBusTransport.cs:17-21`, `AmazonSqsTransport.cs:18-24`). Vendor limits stay inside the transport. SQS caps a message delay at 15 minutes (`MaxDelaySeconds = 900`, `AmazonSqsTransport.cs:46`), so the framework receive pipeline shortens every longer second-level redelivery interval to it (`AmazonSqsTransport.cs:28-30`), and every header travels packed in one message attribute so the 10-attribute cap never applies (`AmazonSqsTransport.cs:24-25`). Service Bus schedules a redelivery arbitrarily far ahead and dead-letters to the queue's native dead-letter queue (`AzureServiceBusTransport.cs:22-24`). The Service Bus transport signs in with a Microsoft Entra token when `MessageBus:AzureServiceBus:FullyQualifiedNamespace` is set, so the namespace can run with shared access keys switched off, and uses the broker connection string otherwise. A connection string carrying `UseDevelopmentEmulator=true` targets the local emulator through [`ServiceBusEmulatorSupport`](#servicebusemulatorsupport) and needs `MessageBus:EmulatorAdminEndpoint` (`AzureServiceBusTransport.cs:27-34`). `[Rubric §10, Messaging & Integration]` asks whether transport choice is isolated from message semantics. Here the semantics live in the core and its shared policies, and the adapter supplies only the wire.

**Identity instead of stored credentials.** Each adapter defaults to the platform's ambient identity and treats a static key as the exception. Azure uses `DefaultAzureCredential` for Blob, Key Vault and the DataProtection blob (`AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:67`, `AzureKeyVaultConfigurationExtensions.cs:117`, `AzureDataProtectionExtensions.cs:77`), and [`AzureTokenCredentials`](#azuretokencredentials) builds the same credential pinned to a user-assigned managed identity when a client id is configured, for Redis and Service Bus (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/AzureTokenCredentials.cs:12`, `:21-23`). GCP uses Application Default Credentials and has no key-file or HMAC setting at all (`GcpServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:38-39`, `:133-135`). AWS falls back to the default credential chain unless `AccessKeyId` and `SecretAccessKey` are both set. [`AwsStaticCredentials`](#awsstaticcredentials) enforces the both-or-neither rule at registration rather than on the first call (`AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:64-65`, `:118-121`, `:187-188`). The three PostgreSQL configurators push the same idea down to the database connection. [`RdsIamAuthenticationConfigurator`](#rdsiamauthenticationconfigurator), [`CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator`](#cloudsqliamauthenticationconfigurator) and [`AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator`](#azurepostgresqlentraauthenticationconfigurator) all implement [`INpgsqlDataSourceConfigurator`](group-07-persistence-ef-core.md#inpgsqldatasourceconfigurator) (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/Rds/RdsIamAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:20-21`, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/CloudSql/CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:23`, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/PostgreSql/AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:23`). Each replaces the password with a short-lived token: an RDS token refreshed every 10 minutes, a Cloud SQL OAuth2 token refreshed every minute, or an Entra token refreshed every four minutes, retried 30 seconds after a failure in every case (`AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:90-93`, `GcpServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:69-73`, `AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:26-29`). Because that token travels as the password, all three registration calls refuse any configured PostgreSQL connection string that does not require TLS, and they refuse it at startup, not on the first query (`AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:123-125`, `GcpServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:101-103`, `AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:193-195`). [`AzureRedisEntraAuthenticationConfigurator`](#azureredisentraauthenticationconfigurator) does the same for every framework Redis connection (the client and distributed cache, the output-cache store, the SignalR backplane and the health check), and a Redis connection string that still carries an access key or sets `ssl=false` fails when its connection is configured (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Redis/AzureRedisEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:21`, `AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:210-226`). `[Rubric §11, Security]` asks whether secrets are kept out of code and configuration and whether credentials are short-lived. These adapters are where the framework answers that question for each cloud.

**Scale-out and observability concerns at the host level.** The three DataProtection calls fix one specific multi-replica failure. The framework default keeps the key ring in memory, so a cookie or antiforgery token minted by one replica cannot be decrypted by another. Persisting the ring to one shared store (an Azure blob, one S3 object, or one Cloud Storage object) gives every replica the same keys (the class summaries above `AzureDataProtectionExtensions.cs:20`, `AwsDataProtectionExtensions.cs:21` and `GoogleCloudDataProtectionExtensions.cs:22`). AWS and GCP build their own repositories. [`S3XmlRepository`](#s3xmlrepository) implements `IXmlRepository` over one S3 object, rewritten read-modify-write under an S3 conditional write (`If-Match` on the ETag it read) so two replicas adding a key at once cannot overwrite each other, and asks for SSE-KMS under a configured key (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/DataProtection/S3XmlRepository.cs:12-28`, class at `:36`). [`AwsDataProtectionBuilderExtensions`](#awsdataprotectionbuilderextensions) plugs it in through `PersistKeysToAmazonS3` (`AwsDataProtectionExtensions.cs:95`). GCP builds its own repository and KMS encryptor and decryptor: [`GcsXmlRepository`](#gcsxmlrepository) implements `IXmlRepository`, and [`GcpKmsXmlEncryptor`](#gcpkmsxmlencryptor) and [`GcpKmsXmlDecryptor`](#gcpkmsxmldecryptor) implement the framework's XML encryption interfaces (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/DataProtection/GcsXmlRepository.cs:31`, `GcpKmsXmlEncryptor.cs:25`, `GcpKmsXmlDecryptor.cs:16`). It rewrites the single object under a generation precondition so two replicas cannot overwrite each other's new key (documented on `GoogleCloudDataProtectionExtensions.cs:77`). Azure and GCP protect the ring with an at-rest key when one is configured (`DataProtection:KeyVaultKeyUri`, `DataProtection:GcpKmsKeyName`), and otherwise register a hosted service that logs one startup Warning, [`AzureKeyRingAtRestNoticeService`](#azurekeyringatrestnoticeservice) and [`GcpKeyRingAtRestNoticeService`](#gcpkeyringatrestnoticeservice) (`AzureDataProtectionExtensions.cs:90-98`, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure.Startup/AzureKeyRingAtRestNoticeService.cs:6-13`, `GoogleCloudDataProtectionExtensions.cs:114-120`, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/DataProtection/GcpKeyRingAtRestNoticeService.cs:14-18`). The three telemetry calls add a vendor exporter beside the vendor-neutral OTLP exporter that `AddServiceDefaults()` configures, and all three must run AFTER it so the service-defaults filter processors see spans first. Each enforces that order even when its gate key is unset, so a misordered host fails on a developer machine (`AzureMonitorExporterExtensions.cs:42-51`, `AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:89-99`, `GoogleCloudTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:101-110`). The Google and AWS exporters send traces and logs only, and metrics go through a collector, because one request is capped at 200 data points on the Google Telemetry API and at 1,000 on AWS (`GoogleCloudTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:78-84`, `AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:66-69`). The AWS exporter writes OTLP to X-Ray and CloudWatch Logs with every request signed by Signature Version 4 in [`AwsSigV4ExportHandler`](#awssigv4exporthandler), and exports logs only when `AwsTelemetry:LogGroup` and `AwsTelemetry:LogStream` are both set (`AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:39-51`, `:252`). Neither exporter fetches a credential at registration. The Google token is fetched by the hosted service [`GoogleCloudTelemetryAccessToken`](#googlecloudtelemetryaccesstoken) at host start (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/Telemetry/GoogleCloudTelemetryAccessToken.cs:28-30`). The AWS default chain is warmed at host start by [`AwsTelemetryCredentialWarmup`](#awstelemetrycredentialwarmup), where a failure is one Warning rather than a failed host, and static keys take [`StaticAwsTelemetryCredentialSource`](#staticawstelemetrycredentialsource) instead of [`DefaultChainAwsTelemetryCredentialSource`](#defaultchainawstelemetrycredentialsource), both behind [`IAwsTelemetryCredentialSource`](#iawstelemetrycredentialsource) (`AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:57-62`, `:124-136`). `[Rubric §13, Observability & Operability]` asks whether telemetry reaches an operator without coupling code to a backend. Here the instrumentation stays vendor-neutral and only the exporter is chosen per cloud.

**Guardrails against a silently missing adapter.** A quiet default is right for a host that never switched a capability on and wrong for one that did. Two startup tripwires close that gap. In the core, the internal `CloudAdapterTripwire` options validator, wired with `ValidateOnStart` in `AddInfrastructure` (`DependencyInjection.cs:243-246`, class at `MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Configuration/CloudAdapterTripwire.cs:32`), fails startup when `NativePush:Enabled` is true but the resolved `INativePushSender` is still `NullNativePushSender`, with a message naming all three push adapter calls and their keys (`CloudAdapterTripwire.cs:77-82`). File storage is deliberately unchecked. A `FileStorage` section is routinely shared across every service of a solution, so it does not declare intent, and a host that does store files without an adapter is not silent anyway, because `NullFileStorageService` fails every upload with `FileStorage.NotConfigured` (`CloudAdapterTripwire.cs:24-28`). In the Aspire package, [`HostIntegrationTripwire`](group-16-aspire-orchestration.md#hostintegrationtripwire) fails startup when configuration asks for a vendor integration whose adapter call left no [`HostIntegrationMarker`](group-16-aspire-orchestration.md#hostintegrationmarker) receipt: `KeyVault:Uri`, `APPLICATIONINSIGHTS_CONNECTION_STRING`, the three DataProtection object keys, both secrets prefixes, `GoogleCloudTelemetry:ProjectId`, `AwsTelemetry:Region` and the boolean token-login gates (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Integrations/HostIntegrationTripwire.cs:15`, keys from `:19`). The adapter calls leave that receipt only past their gate (for example `AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:129-138`, `AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:197-205`, `AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:120-122`), and a host still carrying the retired `DataProtection:SsmParameterPrefix` key fails the same way, naming the S3 key that replaced it (`HostIntegrationTripwire.cs:34-39`). The dependency direction itself is a fitness test. [`CloudVendorIsolationTestsBase`](group-29-testing-infrastructure.md#per-project-test-rollup) asserts that the core references no vendor SDK, beside a compile-time `EnforceCloudVendorBoundary` guard (ADR-134, Decision; restated in `MMCA.Common/AGENTS.md`, "Cloud vendor adapters"). `[Rubric §34, Architecture Governance & Documentation]` asks whether a decision is enforced rather than only written down, and this one is enforced twice.

**Where it is used.** MMCA.ADC is the Azure adopter, and every call it makes comes from `MMCA.Common.Azure` and the `MMCA.Common.Azure.Startup` package it forwards. Every service host, the Gateway and the Blazor web host call `AddAzureKeyVaultConfiguration()` and `AddAzureMonitorExporter()`, the service hosts mostly through the composed service host's `ConfigureConfigurationSources` and `AfterServiceDefaults` hooks (for example `MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Engagement.Service/Program.cs:70,75`, `MMCA.ADC/Source/Hosts/MMCA.ADC.Gateway/Program.cs:75,82`). The four broker services register `AddAzureServiceBusTransport()` through `RegisterBrokerTransports` and call `AddAzureRedisEntraAuthentication` (`MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Conference.Service/Program.cs:228,325`, `MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Engagement.Service/Program.cs:85,140`, `MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Identity.Service/Program.cs:128,260`, `MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Notification.Service/Program.cs:91,97`). Conference and Identity call `AddAzureBlobFileStorage` (`MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Conference.Service/Program.cs:301`, `MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Identity.Service/Program.cs:225`). The Notification module and the Identity host call `AddAzureNotificationHubsPush` (`MMCA.ADC/Source/Modules/Notification/MMCA.ADC.Notification.API/DependencyInjection.cs:40`, `MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Identity.Service/Program.cs:231`). Identity and the Blazor web host call `AddAzureDataProtection()` (`MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Identity.Service/Program.cs:106`, `MMCA.ADC/Source/Hosts/UI/MMCA.ADC.UI.Web/Program.cs:47`). No host under `MMCA.ADC/Source` calls an AWS or GCP adapter, or `AddAzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthentication`. Those are exercised by Common's own LocalStack, emulator and Testcontainers tiers (`aws-integration`, `gcp-integration`, `azure-integration` in `MMCA.Common/AGENTS.md`), not by an MMCA.ADC deployment. The registration calls are C# `extension(T)` members, read as described in the [primer](00-primer.md#c-extensiont-types--read-this-once).

### AwsClientEndpoint
> MMCA.Common.Aws · `MMCA.Common.Aws` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/AwsClientEndpoint.cs:11` · Level 0 · class (internal static)

- **What it is**: a one-method helper that points any AWS SDK `ClientConfig` at either a custom endpoint or an AWS region, from the `ServiceUrl` / `Region` pair every AWS settings section in this package carries.
- **Depends on**: nothing first-party; AWS SDK `ClientConfig` and `RegionEndpoint`.
- **Concept**: one shared rule for endpoint resolution, so SNS, DynamoDB, S3 and Secrets Manager clients interpret the same two keys identically. `[Rubric §15, Best Practices & Code Quality]` assesses whether duplicated logic is factored once; this helper is that single copy.
- **Walkthrough**: `Apply(config, serviceUrl, region)` (`AwsClientEndpoint.cs:23`). An absolute `serviceUrl` wins: it sets `config.ServiceURL` (`AwsClientEndpoint.cs:29`) and, when a region is also given, uses it as the signing region via `AuthenticationRegion` (`AwsClientEndpoint.cs:32`). Otherwise a non-blank region sets `RegionEndpoint` (`AwsClientEndpoint.cs:37`). Neither set leaves the client on the SDK region fallback chain.
- **Why it's built this way**: the doc comment records that the SDK treats the two as mutually exclusive (setting a region endpoint clears the service URL, `AwsClientEndpoint.cs:16-17`), hence the explicit branch rather than setting both. The endpoint override is what lets LocalStack, MinIO and Cloudflare R2 stand in for AWS (`AwsClientEndpoint.cs:13-15`).
- **Where it's used**: [`SnsPushClientFactory`](#snspushclientfactory) (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/Notifications/SnsPushClientFactory.cs:23,39`), [`S3StorageClientFactory`](#s3storageclientfactory), and [`AwsSecretsManagerConfigurationExtensions`](#awssecretsmanagerconfigurationextensions) (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/AwsSecretsManagerConfigurationExtensions.cs:118`). The SQS broker transport uses it too, one step removed: [`AmazonSqsTransport`](#amazonsqstransport) resolves the region and an absolute `ServiceUrl` at registration (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/Messaging/AmazonSqsTransport.cs:79,82`) and hands both to [`AmazonSqsConnection`](#amazonsqsconnection) (`AmazonSqsTransport.cs:87`), which applies them to its SQS and SNS client configs through this helper (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/Messaging/AmazonSqsConnection.cs:50,63`).

### AwsStaticCredentials
> MMCA.Common.Aws · `MMCA.Common.Aws` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/AwsStaticCredentials.cs:10` · Level 0 · class (internal static)

- **What it is**: the shared both-or-neither rule for an `AccessKeyId` / `SecretAccessKey` pair.
- **Depends on**: nothing first-party; AWS SDK `BasicAWSCredentials`.
- **Concept**: a half-configured credential is a configuration error, not a fallback. `[Rubric §11, Security]` assesses whether identity is explicit and least-surprise; the doc comment states the reason directly: silently falling back to the default chain "would run the host under an identity nobody configured" (`AwsStaticCredentials.cs:15-16`).
- **Walkthrough**: `Resolve(accessKeyId, secretAccessKey, sectionPath)` (`AwsStaticCredentials.cs:23`) returns a `BasicAWSCredentials` when both halves are set (`AwsStaticCredentials.cs:28-30`), throws `InvalidOperationException` naming both keys under `sectionPath` when exactly one is set (`AwsStaticCredentials.cs:33-36`), and returns `null` when both are blank (`AwsStaticCredentials.cs:39`), which callers read as "use the AWS default credential chain".
- **Why it's built this way**: the check runs at registration time in every caller, matching [ADR-070](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/070-fail-fast-configuration-contract.html): a misconfiguration surfaces at startup rather than on the first upload, send or query.
- **Where it's used**: [`AwsServiceCollectionExtensions`](#awsservicecollectionextensions) (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:65,118,188`), [`AwsSecretsManagerConfigurationExtensions`](#awssecretsmanagerconfigurationextensions) (`AwsSecretsManagerConfigurationExtensions.cs:97`), [`AmazonSqsTransport`](#amazonsqstransport) (`AmazonSqsTransport.cs:80`) and [`S3StorageClientFactory`](#s3storageclientfactory).

### S3ObjectLocation
> MMCA.Common.Aws · `MMCA.Common.Aws.DataProtection` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/DataProtection/S3ObjectLocation.cs:11` · Level 0 · record (internal sealed, positional)

- **What it is**: the bucket and key of the one S3 object that holds the DataProtection key ring, parsed from the `s3://bucket/key` form of `DataProtection:S3ObjectUri` (`S3ObjectLocation.cs:3-11`).
- **Depends on**: nothing first-party; BCL only.
- **Concept**: no new concept. It is the parse-once configuration value behind the gate-plus-receipt pattern introduced in [`AwsDataProtectionExtensions`](#awsdataprotectionextensions): the string is validated at registration, and every later step receives a typed value it can trust. The Cloud Storage counterpart is [`GcsObjectLocation`](#gcsobjectlocation).
- **Walkthrough**: `Parse(value, configurationKey)` (`S3ObjectLocation.cs:23`) throws `ArgumentNullException` on `null` (`S3ObjectLocation.cs:25`), accepts the `s3://` scheme case-insensitively (`S3ObjectLocation.cs:27`), splits the rest on its first `/`, and returns a location only when the bucket is non-empty, the key is non-empty and the key does not end in `/` (`S3ObjectLocation.cs:29-33`). Anything else throws `InvalidOperationException` naming the configuration key and the rejected value (`S3ObjectLocation.cs:37-38`). `ToString()` rebuilds the `s3://bucket/key` form (`S3ObjectLocation.cs:42`), which [`S3XmlRepository`](#s3xmlrepository) puts in its write-failure message.
- **Why it's built this way**: the value is parsed by hand rather than through `Uri`, because `Uri` lower-cases and re-escapes an unknown scheme's authority and path in ways a bucket name or object key must not be (`S3ObjectLocation.cs:5-7`). A key ending in `/` is rejected because it names a folder, not an object (`S3ObjectLocation.cs:16-17`).
- **Where it's used**: [`AwsDataProtectionExtensions`](#awsdataprotectionextensions) parses it before registering anything (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/AwsDataProtectionExtensions.cs:84`), [`AwsDataProtectionBuilderExtensions`](#awsdataprotectionbuilderextensions) takes it as a parameter (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/DataProtection/AwsDataProtectionBuilderExtensions.cs:32`), and [`S3XmlRepository`](#s3xmlrepository) holds it as `Location` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/DataProtection/S3XmlRepository.cs:64`). Covered by `MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws.Tests/DataProtection/S3ObjectLocationTests.cs`.

### AwsDataProtectionExtensions
> MMCA.Common.Aws · `MMCA.Common.Aws` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/AwsDataProtectionExtensions.cs:21` · Level 1 · class (static, `extension<TBuilder>`)

- **What it is**: `AddAwsDataProtection()`, which persists the ASP.NET Core DataProtection key ring to one Amazon S3 object so every replica of a scaled-out host shares one key ring (`AwsDataProtectionExtensions.cs:30-31`).
- **Depends on**: [`HostIntegrationMarker`](group-16-aspire-orchestration.md#hostintegrationmarker) and [`HostIntegrationNames`](group-16-aspire-orchestration.md#hostintegrationnames); the internal [`S3ObjectLocation`](#s3objectlocation) (parses the `s3://bucket/key` form), [`AwsDataProtectionBuilderExtensions`](#awsdataprotectionbuilderextensions) (`PersistKeysToAmazonS3`) and [`S3XmlRepository`](#s3xmlrepository); externals `IHostApplicationBuilder`, `AddDataProtection`, and `IAmazonS3` from `AWSSDK.S3`, with no DataProtection package of AWS's own.
- **Concept**: a configuration-gated host integration with a startup receipt. The method is a no-op unless its gate key is set; when it does run, it registers a marker the core startup tripwire looks for, so a host that has the key but forgot the call fails instead of running every replica on its own key ring (`AwsDataProtectionExtensions.cs:86-88`). It is declared as a C# `extension<TBuilder>` block (`AwsDataProtectionExtensions.cs:26-27`; see [C# `extension(T)` types](00-primer.md#c-extensiont-types--read-this-once)). `[Rubric §29, Resilience & Business Continuity]` assesses whether scale-out does not break user sessions; a shared key ring is what keeps antiforgery tokens and auth cookies valid across replicas.
- **Walkthrough**: reads `DataProtection:S3ObjectUri` (`AwsDataProtectionExtensions.cs:23`, `:72`) and returns unchanged when blank (`AwsDataProtectionExtensions.cs:78-81`), deliberately registering no receipt so an object configured by a later source still trips the check (`AwsDataProtectionExtensions.cs:74-77`). Otherwise it parses the URI through [`S3ObjectLocation`](#s3objectlocation) first, failing with the key named before anything is registered (`AwsDataProtectionExtensions.cs:83-84`), registers `HostIntegrationMarker(HostIntegrationNames.AwsDataProtection)` (`AwsDataProtectionExtensions.cs:88`), resolves the application name from `DataProtection:ApplicationName` or the host's `ApplicationName` (`AwsDataProtectionExtensions.cs:90-91`), and calls `PersistKeysToAmazonS3(location, DataProtection:KmsKeyId)` (`AwsDataProtectionExtensions.cs:93-95`). That step sets the key manager's `XmlRepository` to an `S3XmlRepository` built when DataProtection first reads its options, over the `IAmazonS3` the host registered or, failing that, a default client from the SDK default region and credential chain (`AwsDataProtectionBuilderExtensions.cs:34-40`).
- **Why it's built this way**: the whole ring is one XML object, the shape the Azure Blob and Cloud Storage repositories use, written read-modify-write under an S3 conditional write: `If-Match` on the ETag it read, or `If-None-Match: *` to create it, so two replicas adding a key at once cannot overwrite each other; the loser re-reads and retries up to 10 times with a linear backoff (`S3XmlRepository.cs:16-23`, `:39`, `:101`, `:105`; detail in [`S3XmlRepository`](#s3xmlrepository)). `DataProtection:KmsKeyId` selects SSE-KMS for every write; without it the bucket's default encryption applies (`AwsDataProtectionExtensions.cs:40-42`). The gate keeps developer machines and test hosts on the in-memory default with no AWS dependency at startup (`AwsDataProtectionExtensions.cs:35-39`). The doc comment states that setting this object together with `DataProtection:BlobStorageUri` or `DataProtection:GcsObjectUri` fails the startup tripwire (`AwsDataProtectionExtensions.cs:61-63`), one key ring per host, and the tripwire also fails a host that still sets the retired `DataProtection:SsmParameterPrefix`, naming `DataProtection:S3ObjectUri` as the replacement (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Integrations/HostIntegrationTripwire.cs:123-126`; see [HostIntegrationTripwire](group-16-aspire-orchestration.md#hostintegrationtripwire)). Recorded in [ADR-069](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/069-shared-data-protection-key-ring.html) and [ADR-134](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/134-cloud-neutral-core-vendor-adapters.html); the Azure sibling is [`AzureDataProtectionExtensions`](#azuredataprotectionextensions) and the Google Cloud sibling is [`GoogleCloudDataProtectionExtensions`](#googleclouddataprotectionextensions).
- **Where it's used**: no call site outside the defining file; no `MMCA.ADC/Source` host calls it. Covered by `MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws.Tests/AwsDataProtectionExtensionsTests.cs`.
- **Caveats / not-in-source**: the required IAM permissions (`s3:GetObject` and `s3:PutObject` on the object, `s3:ListBucket` on the bucket, plus `kms:GenerateDataKey` and `kms:Decrypt` when a KMS key is set) are stated in the doc comment (`AwsDataProtectionExtensions.cs:54-58`) and are not checked by code; without `s3:ListBucket` a not-yet-created object reads as 403 and the first start fails.

### S3XmlRepository
> MMCA.Common.Aws · `MMCA.Common.Aws.DataProtection` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/DataProtection/S3XmlRepository.cs:36` · Level 1 · class (internal sealed, `IXmlRepository`)

- **What it is**: the DataProtection `IXmlRepository` that keeps the whole key ring as ONE S3 object: a `<repository>` document whose children are the key elements (`S3XmlRepository.cs:11-14`, `:41`).
- **Depends on**: [`S3ObjectLocation`](#s3objectlocation); externals `IXmlRepository` (`Microsoft.AspNetCore.DataProtection.Repositories`), `IAmazonS3`, `GetObjectRequest`, `PutObjectRequest` and `AmazonS3Exception` from `AWSSDK.S3`, and `System.Xml.Linq`.
- **Concept introduced, optimistic concurrency through a conditional write.** Several replicas may each decide to add a key at the same moment. Instead of a lock, every write states the version it is based on: a read returns the content and its ETag in one response, and the write carries `If-Match` with that ETag (or `If-None-Match: *` when the object did not exist yet, so the write can only create it). If another replica wrote in between, S3 refuses with 412 (or 409 when it sees the two conditional writes overlap), and the loser re-reads the ring, which now holds the winner's key, and tries again (`S3XmlRepository.cs:16-23`). Neither key is lost. This is the S3 counterpart of the Blob ETag and the Cloud Storage generation precondition; the Cloud Storage sibling is [`GcsXmlRepository`](#gcsxmlrepository). The rubric tag for the shared key ring sits on [`AwsDataProtectionExtensions`](#awsdataprotectionextensions).
- **Walkthrough**:
  - Constants: `MaxAttempts = 10` (`S3XmlRepository.cs:39`), the root element name `repository` (`S3XmlRepository.cs:41`), and UTF-8 writer settings without a byte order mark, so the object is plain XML text (`S3XmlRepository.cs:43-44`).
  - The constructor (`S3XmlRepository.cs:55-61`) null-checks the client and location and normalizes a blank KMS key id to `null` (`S3XmlRepository.cs:59`).
  - `GetAllElements()` (`S3XmlRepository.cs:67-68`) reads the document and returns the root's child elements.
  - `StoreElement(element, friendlyName)` (`S3XmlRepository.cs:71`) loops up to `MaxAttempts` (`S3XmlRepository.cs:75`): read the document and ETag (`S3XmlRepository.cs:77`), append a copy of the element so the caller's element is never re-parented into a document a retry discards (`S3XmlRepository.cs:79-80`), serialize it (`S3XmlRepository.cs:82-88`), and build a `PutObjectRequest` with `ContentType = application/xml` (`S3XmlRepository.cs:90-97`). The condition is `IfNoneMatch = "*"` when there was no ETag and `IfMatch = eTag` otherwise (`S3XmlRepository.cs:99-106`); a configured KMS key adds SSE-KMS (`S3XmlRepository.cs:108-112`). A 412 or 409 is caught (`S3XmlRepository.cs:119`) and the thread sleeps `retryDelay * attempt` before the next attempt, skipping the sleep after the last one (`S3XmlRepository.cs:123-126`). When every attempt loses, it throws `InvalidOperationException` naming the location and the attempt count (`S3XmlRepository.cs:130-131`).
  - `ReadAsync()` (`S3XmlRepository.cs:143`) returns the loaded document with the response ETag (`S3XmlRepository.cs:147-151`); a 404 yields an empty `<repository>` with a `null` ETag, so the first write is create-only (`S3XmlRepository.cs:153-156`).
  - `Wait<T>` (`S3XmlRepository.cs:140`) runs the SDK call on the thread pool and blocks on it, with `VSTHRD002` suppressed (`S3XmlRepository.cs:139-141`), so a caller holding a synchronization context (a Blazor circuit) cannot deadlock it (`S3XmlRepository.cs:134-137`).
- **Why it's built this way**: one object per ring rather than one per key matches the Blob and Cloud Storage repositories (`S3XmlRepository.cs:11-13`). Reading content and ETag from the same response guarantees the ETag a write is conditioned on is the one whose content it extended (`S3XmlRepository.cs:22-23`). Blocking sync-over-async is accepted because `IXmlRepository` is synchronous, the SDK is async only, and DataProtection reads the ring rarely (at startup and on its refresh period) and writes it only when it creates a key (`S3XmlRepository.cs:30-33`). See [ADR-069](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/069-shared-data-protection-key-ring.html).
- **Where it's used**: constructed only by [`AwsDataProtectionBuilderExtensions`](#awsdataprotectionbuilderextensions) (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/DataProtection/AwsDataProtectionBuilderExtensions.cs:36-40`). Covered by `MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws.Tests/DataProtection/S3XmlRepositoryTests.cs` and `MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws.Tests/AwsDataProtectionExtensionsTests.cs`.
- **Caveats / not-in-source**: the backoff is `Thread.Sleep`, so a contended write blocks its thread. With the 50 ms unit the builder passes (`AwsDataProtectionBuilderExtensions.cs:18`), nine sleeps of 1 to 9 units add up to 2.25 s before the final failure; that figure is arithmetic on the cited values, not a measured number.

### AwsDataProtectionBuilderExtensions
> MMCA.Common.Aws · `MMCA.Common.Aws.DataProtection` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/DataProtection/AwsDataProtectionBuilderExtensions.cs:15` · Level 2 · class (internal static, classic `this` extension)

- **What it is**: the internal `PersistKeysToAmazonS3(location, kmsKeyId)` step behind `AddAwsDataProtection`, shaped like the framework's own `PersistKeysTo...` calls (`AwsDataProtectionBuilderExtensions.cs:9-13`, `:32`).
- **Depends on**: [`S3ObjectLocation`](#s3objectlocation), [`S3XmlRepository`](#s3xmlrepository); externals `IDataProtectionBuilder`, `KeyManagementOptions`, `IConfigureOptions<T>` / `ConfigureOptions<T>`, `IAmazonS3` and `AmazonS3Client`.
- **Concept**: no new concept. It defers client resolution through the options system: instead of building the repository at registration, it registers an `IConfigureOptions<KeyManagementOptions>` that runs when DataProtection first builds its options (`AwsDataProtectionBuilderExtensions.cs:11-13`). The Google Cloud sibling is [`GcpDataProtectionBuilderExtensions`](#gcpdataprotectionbuilderextensions).
- **Walkthrough**: `RetryDelay` is 50 ms, the backoff unit handed to the repository (`AwsDataProtectionBuilderExtensions.cs:18`). `PersistKeysToAmazonS3` (`AwsDataProtectionBuilderExtensions.cs:32`) adds a singleton options configurator (`AwsDataProtectionBuilderExtensions.cs:34-35`) that sets `XmlRepository` to a new [`S3XmlRepository`](#s3xmlrepository) over the container's `IAmazonS3`, or a new `AmazonS3Client()` from the SDK defaults when there is none, plus the location, KMS key id and delay (`AwsDataProtectionBuilderExtensions.cs:36-40`), then returns the builder (`AwsDataProtectionBuilderExtensions.cs:42`).
- **Why it's built this way**: the fallback client is deliberately NOT added to the container: a default client registered here would win the `TryAdd` in a later `AddS3FileStorage` and silently ignore its `FileStorage` endpoint settings (`AwsDataProtectionBuilderExtensions.cs:24-26`). Resolving at options time also means the host's own client (an emulator endpoint, or the one `AddS3FileStorage` registers, `AwsDataProtectionBuilderExtensions.cs:21-23`) is found regardless of registration order. See [ADR-069](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/069-shared-data-protection-key-ring.html).
- **Where it's used**: only [`AwsDataProtectionExtensions`](#awsdataprotectionextensions) (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/AwsDataProtectionExtensions.cs:95`).

### AwsSecretsManagerConfigurationExtensions
> MMCA.Common.Aws · `MMCA.Common.Aws` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/AwsSecretsManagerConfigurationExtensions.cs:23` · Level 2 · class (static, `extension<TBuilder>`)

- **What it is**: `AddAwsSecretsManagerConfiguration()`, which adds an AWS Secrets Manager configuration source so every secret under one name prefix reads through `IConfiguration` like any other setting.
- **Depends on**: [`AwsSecretsManagerConfigurationSource`](#awssecretsmanagerconfigurationsource), [`AwsStaticCredentials`](#awsstaticcredentials), [`AwsClientEndpoint`](#awsclientendpoint), [`HostIntegrationMarker`](group-16-aspire-orchestration.md#hostintegrationmarker), [`HostIntegrationNames`](group-16-aspire-orchestration.md#hostintegrationnames); AWS SDK `AmazonSecretsManagerClient`.
- **Concept**: runtime secrets as a configuration source, with the same gate-plus-receipt shape as [`AwsDataProtectionExtensions`](#awsdataprotectionextensions). `[Rubric §11, Security]` assesses whether secrets stay out of files and images; here production secrets are fetched from the vault at startup and optionally re-read on an interval.
- **Walkthrough**: reads `SecretsManager:NamePrefix` (`AwsSecretsManagerConfigurationExtensions.cs:85`) and returns unchanged when blank, registering no receipt (`AwsSecretsManagerConfigurationExtensions.cs:87-94`). It then resolves the static key pair, failing before any source is added (`AwsSecretsManagerConfigurationExtensions.cs:96-98`). `ReloadIntervalMinutes`, when present, must parse as a positive invariant-culture integer or the method throws naming the value (`AwsSecretsManagerConfigurationExtensions.cs:103-115`). It builds an `AmazonSecretsManagerConfig` through `AwsClientEndpoint.Apply` (`AwsSecretsManagerConfigurationExtensions.cs:117-121`), registers `HostIntegrationMarker(HostIntegrationNames.AwsSecretsManagerConfiguration)` (`AwsSecretsManagerConfigurationExtensions.cs:125`), and adds the source with a client factory, the prefix and the interval (`AwsSecretsManagerConfigurationExtensions.cs:127-130`).
- **Why it's built this way**: the naming convention matches the Key Vault sibling ([`AzureKeyVaultConfigurationExtensions`](#azurekeyvaultconfigurationextensions)): the prefix is stripped and `--` maps to the configuration separator, so `adc/prod/ConnectionStrings--Default` arrives as `ConnectionStrings:Default` (`AwsSecretsManagerConfigurationExtensions.cs:53-57`), letting a secret set move between clouds by renaming its prefix. A malformed reload interval fails loudly rather than degrading to "never reload" (`AwsSecretsManagerConfigurationExtensions.cs:100-102`). It is deliberately not called from `AddServiceDefaults()`, and because `ConfigurationManager` loads each source as it is added the secrets are read synchronously at this call, so it must run before the settings binding that reads them (`AwsSecretsManagerConfigurationExtensions.cs:67-73`). See [ADR-061](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/061-runtime-secret-management.html) and [ADR-134](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/134-cloud-neutral-core-vendor-adapters.html).
- **Where it's used**: no call site outside the defining file; no `MMCA.ADC/Source` host calls it.
- **Caveats / not-in-source**: the claim that a secret the identity cannot read fails startup (`AwsSecretsManagerConfigurationExtensions.cs:63-64`) is enforced in the configuration provider, not in this file.

### AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions
> MMCA.Common.Aws · `MMCA.Common.Aws` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:22` · Level 2 · class (static, `extension<TBuilder>`)

- **What it is**: `AddAwsTelemetryExporter()`, which exports traces, and logs when a log group and stream are configured, straight to the AWS X-Ray and CloudWatch Logs OTLP endpoints, signed with AWS Signature Version 4, beside the vendor-neutral OTLP exporter `AddServiceDefaults()` configures. It is the AWS twin of `AddAzureMonitorExporter()` and `AddGoogleCloudTelemetryExporter()`, with no collector sidecar (`AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:16-21`, `:40-43`).
- **Depends on**: [`AwsTelemetrySettings`](#awstelemetrysettings), [`AwsStaticCredentials`](#awsstaticcredentials), [`IAwsTelemetryCredentialSource`](#iawstelemetrycredentialsource), [`DefaultChainAwsTelemetryCredentialSource`](#defaultchainawstelemetrycredentialsource), [`StaticAwsTelemetryCredentialSource`](#staticawstelemetrycredentialsource), [`AwsTelemetryCredentialWarmup`](#awstelemetrycredentialwarmup), [`AwsSigV4ExportHandler`](#awssigv4exporthandler), [`HostIntegrationMarker`](group-16-aspire-orchestration.md#hostintegrationmarker), [`HostIntegrationNames`](group-16-aspire-orchestration.md#hostintegrationnames); externals OpenTelemetry `OtlpTraceExporter`, `OtlpLogExporter`, `BatchActivityExportProcessor`, `BatchLogRecordExportProcessor`, and AWS SDK `RegionEndpoint`.
- **Concept**: the gate-plus-receipt host integration introduced in [`AwsDataProtectionExtensions`](#awsdataprotectionextensions), applied to telemetry export, plus an ordering guard. `[Rubric §13, Observability & Operability]` assesses whether telemetry reaches the operator's backend in a usable shape; here traces and logs land in X-Ray and CloudWatch Logs directly, stamped with `cloud.provider = aws` and `cloud.region` resource attributes (`AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:143-147`). See [ADR-041](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/041-observability-and-telemetry.html).
- **Walkthrough**:
  - Constants: the SigV4 service names `xray` and `logs` (`AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:25`, `:28`) and the `x-aws-log-group` / `x-aws-log-stream` header names (`AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:31`, `:34`).
  - `AddAwsTelemetryExporter()` (`AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:89`) first throws unless the non-keyed `HostIntegrationNames.ServiceDefaultsTelemetry` marker is already registered, whether or not the region is set (`AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:91-99`). It reads [`AwsTelemetrySettings`](#awstelemetrysettings) (`AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:101`) and returns with no exporter, no credential lookup and no receipt when `AwsTelemetry:Region` is blank (`AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:103-108`).
  - Past the gate it resolves the region (`AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:110-111`), the both-or-neither static key pair (`AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:112-115`), the both-or-neither log group and stream (`AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:116`, `:212-223`) and the two endpoints (`AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:117-118`), then registers `HostIntegrationMarker(HostIntegrationNames.AwsTelemetryExporter)` (`AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:122`).
  - It picks a static or default-chain credential source (`AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:124-126`); on the default chain it registers [`AwsTelemetryCredentialWarmup`](#awstelemetrycredentialwarmup) as a singleton and a hosted service (`AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:130-136`).
  - It adds the resource attributes and a batch trace processor over an `OtlpTraceExporter` (`AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:142-150`), and, only when logs are configured, a batch log processor over an `OtlpLogExporter` that adds the log group and stream headers (`AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:152-162`).
  - `ResolveTracesEndpoint` and `ResolveLogsEndpoint` (`AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:176-177`, `:187-188`) take a configured override as the full signal URL, which must be an absolute http or https URL or the call throws naming the key, and otherwise default to `https://xray.{region}.amazonaws.com/v1/traces` and `https://logs.{region}.amazonaws.com/v1/logs` (`AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:190-208`).
  - `CreateExporterOptions` (`AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:234`) sets OTLP over HTTP/protobuf with `Headers = null` (`AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:244-249`) and an `HttpClientFactory` that wraps an [`AwsSigV4ExportHandler`](#awssigv4exporthandler) over an `HttpClientHandler`, keeping the exporter timeout and the container's `TimeProvider` or `TimeProvider.System` (`AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:242-258`).
- **Why it's built this way**: the exporters are built by hand rather than through `AddOtlpExporter`, because the OpenTelemetry SDK refuses a signal-specific `AddOtlpExporter` where `UseOtlpExporter` (the vendor-neutral exporter service defaults enable) also runs, and this one has to coexist with it (`AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:138-141`). Clearing the headers keeps an `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_HEADERS` meant for the vendor-neutral exporter from being sent to AWS (`AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:229-232`). The ordering guard exists because the outbox-poll and probe filter processors service defaults register must run before this exporter's batch processor, or the spans they un-record are exported anyway (`AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:77-80`). Metrics are not exported: one request is capped at 1,000 data points and the OpenTelemetry .NET SDK offers no supported way to split one metric's points, so metrics go through an ADOT collector via `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT` (`AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:66-69`). The default endpoints are for the commercial `aws` partition; China and GovCloud hosts set the overrides (`AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:55-56`). The default credential chain is resolved once while the host starts, never at registration, and a failure there is logged as one Warning without failing the host (`AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:58-61`). `AwsTelemetry:Region` set without this call fails the startup tripwire (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Integrations/HostIntegrationTripwire.cs:56`, `:145-146`). Recorded in [ADR-041](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/041-observability-and-telemetry.html) and [ADR-134](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/134-cloud-neutral-core-vendor-adapters.html); siblings [`AzureMonitorExporterExtensions`](#azuremonitorexporterextensions) and [`GoogleCloudTelemetryExporterExtensions`](#googlecloudtelemetryexporterextensions).
- **Where it's used**: [`AwsSigV4ExportHandler`](#awssigv4exporthandler) reads the log group and stream header constants (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/Telemetry/AwsSigV4ExportHandler.cs:38-39`); no `MMCA.ADC/Source` host calls the method. Covered by `MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws.Tests/Telemetry/AwsTelemetryExporterExtensionsTests.cs`.
- **Caveats / not-in-source**: the doc comment states that the signature, headers, gating and wiring are covered by tests against the SDK signer and a loopback receiver, and that the call has not been exercised against live X-Ray or CloudWatch Logs endpoints (`AwsTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:72-74`).

### AwsServiceCollectionExtensions
> MMCA.Common.Aws · `MMCA.Common.Aws` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:30` · Level 7 · class (static, `extension(IServiceCollection)`)

- **What it is**: the public DI surface of the AWS adapter package: `AddS3FileStorage`, `AddAmazonSqsTransport`, `AddAwsRdsIamAuthentication` and `AddAmazonSnsPush`, each replacing a provider-neutral core default with an AWS implementation.
- **Depends on**: [`S3FileStorageSettings`](#s3filestoragesettings), [`S3StorageClientFactory`](#s3storageclientfactory), [`S3FileStorageService`](#s3filestorageservice), [`IFileStorageService`](group-07-persistence-ef-core.md#ifilestorageservice), [`AmazonSqsTransport`](#amazonsqstransport), [`RdsIamAuthenticationSettings`](#rdsiamauthenticationsettings), [`RdsIamAuthenticationConfigurator`](#rdsiamauthenticationconfigurator), [`SnsNativePushSettings`](#snsnativepushsettings), [`SnsPushClientFactory`](#snspushclientfactory), [`SnsInstallationRegistry`](#snsinstallationregistry), [`SnsNativePushSender`](#snsnativepushsender), [`SnsPushDeviceRegistrar`](#snspushdeviceregistrar), [`INativePushSender`](group-07-persistence-ef-core.md#inativepushsender), [`IPushDeviceRegistrar`](group-07-persistence-ef-core.md#ipushdeviceregistrar), [`AwsStaticCredentials`](#awsstaticcredentials), [`HostIntegrationMarker`](group-16-aspire-orchestration.md#hostintegrationmarker), [`HostIntegrationNames`](group-16-aspire-orchestration.md#hostintegrationnames).
- **Concept introduced, the cloud-neutral core with vendor adapters.** Core Common defines the abstractions and a safe default (an unconfigured file store, a null push sender, the broker transport registry, the Npgsql configurator hook); this package swaps in AWS implementations only when called and only when configured. Every method is configuration-gated, so a host can call it unconditionally and a deployment turns the capability on by configuration alone. Declared as a C# `extension(IServiceCollection)` block (`AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:32`; see [C# `extension(T)` types](00-primer.md#c-extensiont-types--read-this-once), [ADR-106](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/106-extension-members-as-public-di-surface.html)). `[Rubric §7, Microservices Readiness]` assesses vendor independence of the core; [ADR-134](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/134-cloud-neutral-core-vendor-adapters.html) records the split, and `ArchitectureRules.CloudVendors` enforces it (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Testing.Architecture/Rules/Layering/ArchitectureRules.CloudVendors.cs:98-99`). `[Rubric §1, SOLID]` (open/closed): new providers arrive as packages, not core edits. Sibling surfaces: [`AzureServiceCollectionExtensions`](#azureservicecollectionextensions), [`GcpServiceCollectionExtensions`](#gcpservicecollectionextensions).
- **Walkthrough**:
  - `AddS3FileStorage(configuration)` (`AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:51`) binds `FileStorage` options (`AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:55-56`), is a no-op without a `Bucket` (`AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:58-62`), checks the key pair at registration (`AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:65`), then `TryAddSingleton`s the S3 client and adds `IFileStorageService` as `S3FileStorageService` (`AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:67-68`). Checksum settings default to `WHEN_REQUIRED` because SDK v4 sends integrity checksums by default and several S3-compatible services reject them (`AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:42-43`). See [ADR-045](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/045-managed-file-storage-and-avatars.html).
  - `AddAmazonSqsTransport()` (`AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:86-87`) registers a new [`AmazonSqsTransport`](#amazonsqstransport) through `AddBrokerTransport`; it must be called before `AddBrokerMessaging`, which picks the transport (`AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:75-76`). Its doc comment records that SQS caps a message delay at 15 minutes, so every second-level redelivery interval longer than that is shortened to it, and that with `MessageBus:ProvisionTopology` on (the default) the transport creates the SNS topics, the host's queue, its `{queue}-error` queue and the subscriptions itself (`AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:79-82`). See [ADR-066](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/066-broker-transport-selection.html).
  - `AddAwsRdsIamAuthentication(configuration)` (`AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:108`) is a no-op unless `Rds:IamAuthentication:Enabled` is true (`AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:112-116`), resolves credentials (`AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:118-121`), fails on any configured cleartext PostgreSQL connection string (`AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:123-125`), resolves the optional region (`AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:127`), registers the `HostIntegrationMarker(HostIntegrationNames.AwsRdsIamAuthentication)` receipt only past the gate and only once, so the gate set without this call fails the startup tripwire instead of logging in with whatever password the connection string carries (`AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:129-138`), and calls `AddNpgsqlDataSourceConfigurator` with a [`RdsIamAuthenticationConfigurator`](#rdsiamauthenticationconfigurator) (`AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:140`).
  - `AddAmazonSnsPush(configuration)` (`AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:174`) is a no-op unless `NativePush:Enabled` parses true and the section `IsComplete` (`AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:178-185`); it then resolves credentials (`AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:188`), binds options (`AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:190`), `TryAddSingleton`s the SNS and DynamoDB clients and the registry (`AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:191-193`), and adds the sender and registrar as transients (`AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:194-195`). See [ADR-044](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/044-native-push-delivery.html).
- **Why it's built this way**: `TryAddSingleton` for SDK clients means a client the host registered first is used as is, the same posture as the Azure adapter (`AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:45-46,167-168`). Every key-pair check runs at registration, not on first use ([ADR-070](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/070-fail-fast-configuration-contract.html)). The configuration-pipeline integrations (secrets, data protection) live on the host builder instead, in [`AwsSecretsManagerConfigurationExtensions`](#awssecretsmanagerconfigurationextensions) and [`AwsDataProtectionExtensions`](#awsdataprotectionextensions), because they act on `builder.Configuration`.
- **Where it's used**: no call site outside the defining file; no `MMCA.ADC/Source` host calls any of the four methods (ADC runs on Azure). Tests call them, for example `MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws.Tests/Notifications/AddAmazonSnsPushTests.cs`.
- **Caveats / not-in-source**: the IAM permissions listed for SNS push (`AwsServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:161-168`) and the external provisioning of the table and platform applications are documentation only; no code verifies them.

### AmazonSqsTransportSettings
> MMCA.Common.Aws · `MMCA.Common.Aws.Messaging` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/Messaging/AmazonSqsTransportSettings.cs:8` · Level 0 · class

- **What it is**: the bound options class for the Amazon SQS/SNS broker transport, read from the `MessageBus:AmazonSqs` section (`AmazonSqsTransportSettings.cs:11`).
- **Depends on**: nothing first-party; `System.Uri` for the endpoint override.
- **Concept**: a vendor-specific sub-section of the provider-neutral [`MessageBusSettings`](group-14-module-system-composition.md#messagebussettings). The neutral settings carry the redelivery policy; this class carries only what SQS addressing needs. `[Rubric §10, Messaging & Integration]` assesses whether broker choice is a configuration concern rather than a code change; here the SQS addressing lives in its own section so the shared `MessageBus` keys stay transport-agnostic.
- **Walkthrough**: four `init` properties. `Region` (`AmazonSqsTransportSettings.cs:18`) is a region system name; blank defers to the AWS SDK fallback chain, and no region at all fails registration (enforced by [`AmazonSqsTransport`](#amazonsqstransport)). `ServiceUrl` (`AmazonSqsTransportSettings.cs:24`) is one endpoint override for both SQS and SNS, honored only when absolute. `AccessKeyId` / `SecretAccessKey` (`AmazonSqsTransportSettings.cs:31,34`) are an optional static key pair, both-or-neither (enforced by [`AwsStaticCredentials`](#awsstaticcredentials)).
- **Why it's built this way**: every field is optional because the AWS default chains (region and credentials) are the production path for an IAM-role host; the static pair and endpoint override exist for LocalStack and non-AWS environments. Transport selection by configuration is recorded in [ADR-066](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/066-broker-transport-selection.html), the fail-at-registration posture in [ADR-070](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/070-fail-fast-configuration-contract.html).
- **Where it's used**: read once inside [`AmazonSqsTransport`](#amazonsqstransport)`.Configure` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/Messaging/AmazonSqsTransport.cs:78-79`) and named in its error messages (`AmazonSqsTransport.cs:81,152`).

### IAmazonSqsSettlement
> MMCA.Common.Aws · `MMCA.Common.Aws.Messaging` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/Messaging/IAmazonSqsSettlement.cs:8` · Level 0 · interface (internal)

- **What it is**: the four SQS calls that settle one received message, abstracted so the settle logic in [`AmazonSqsDeliveryHandler`](#amazonsqsdeliveryhandler) never touches the SDK client directly.
- **Depends on**: nothing first-party; BCL `Task`, `CancellationToken`, `IReadOnlyDictionary<string, string>`.
- **Concept**: a settlement port, the SQS counterpart of [`IAzureServiceBusSettlement`](#iazureservicebussettlement). `[Rubric §14, Testability]` assesses whether behavior can be exercised without real infrastructure; with the broker calls behind this interface, every disposition branch of the handler is tested against a fake (`MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws.Tests/Messaging/AmazonSqsDeliveryHandlerTests.cs`).
- **Walkthrough**: `DeleteAsync` removes the received message from the host's queue (`IAmazonSqsSettlement.cs:13`). `SendToQueueAsync` sends a copy back to the same queue with a delay of 0 to 900 seconds (`IAmazonSqsSettlement.cs:21`). `SendToErrorQueueAsync` sends a copy to the `{queue}-error` queue (`IAmazonSqsSettlement.cs:28`). `ChangeVisibilityAsync` resets the message's visibility timeout from now: a heartbeat during processing, or zero to hand it straight back to the queue (`IAmazonSqsSettlement.cs:37`). Headers are passed as a dictionary and packed into one attribute by the implementation (`IAmazonSqsSettlement.cs:17,25`).
- **Why it's built this way**: SQS has no native dead-letter or defer verb per message, so every disposition is composed from these primitives (send a copy, then delete). Keeping that composition in the handler and only the raw calls here makes the order of operations (copy first, delete second) testable in isolation.
- **Where it's used**: implemented by [`QueueSettlement`](#queuesettlement) (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/Messaging/AmazonSqsReceiver.cs:273`), consumed by [`AmazonSqsDeliveryHandler`](#amazonsqsdeliveryhandler)`.HandleAsync` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/Messaging/AmazonSqsDeliveryHandler.cs:62`).

### AmazonSqsConnection
> MMCA.Common.Aws · `MMCA.Common.Aws.Messaging` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/Messaging/AmazonSqsConnection.cs:13` · Level 1 · class (internal sealed)

- **What it is**: the owner of the one `AmazonSQSClient` and one `AmazonSimpleNotificationServiceClient` the transport uses, registered as a singleton so both clients are disposed with the container.
- **Depends on**: [`AwsClientEndpoint`](#awsclientendpoint); AWS SDK `AmazonSQSClient`, `AmazonSimpleNotificationServiceClient`, `RegionEndpoint`, `AWSCredentials`.
- **Concept**: a connection holder shared by the publisher, the provisioner and the receiver; see [`AzureServiceBusConnection`](#azureservicebusconnection) for the Azure sibling.
- **Walkthrough**: the constructor builds an SQS and an SNS config, then creates each client with static credentials when supplied, else with the AWS default credential chain (`AmazonSqsConnection.cs:22-33`). `Sqs` and `Sns` expose the clients as their interfaces (`AmazonSqsConnection.cs:36,39`). `CreateSqsConfig` and `CreateSnsConfig` each start from an empty SDK config and hand it to `AwsClientEndpoint.Apply` with the optional endpoint override and the region's system name (`AmazonSqsConnection.cs:45-52,58-65`). `Dispose` disposes both clients (`AmazonSqsConnection.cs:68-72`).
- **Why it's built this way**: one endpoint override serves both services, which is what LocalStack needs, and the config factories are `internal static` so tests can assert the endpoint and signing region without opening a connection.
- **Where it's used**: registered by [`AmazonSqsTransport`](#amazonsqstransport)`.Register` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/Messaging/AmazonSqsTransport.cs:87`) and resolved there for the provisioner, publisher and receiver (`AmazonSqsTransport.cs:91,97,101`); covered by `MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws.Tests/Messaging/AmazonSqsTransportTests.cs`.

### AmazonSqsEntityNames
> MMCA.Common.Aws · `MMCA.Common.Aws.Messaging` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/Messaging/AmazonSqsEntityNames.cs:12` · Level 3 · class (internal static)

- **What it is**: the naming rules that turn the framework's topic and queue names into legal SNS topic and SQS queue names.
- **Depends on**: [`BrokerNaming`](group-14-module-system-composition.md#brokernaming).
- **Concept**: per-vendor name fitting over the neutral [`BrokerNaming`](group-14-module-system-composition.md#brokernaming)`.Fit`; the Azure sibling is [`AzureServiceBusEntityNames`](#azureservicebusentitynames).
- **Walkthrough**: three constants: `MaxTopicNameLength = 256` (`AmazonSqsEntityNames.cs:15`), `MaxQueueNameLength = 80` (`AmazonSqsEntityNames.cs:18`) and `ErrorQueueSuffix = "-error"` (`AmazonSqsEntityNames.cs:21`). `Topic` fits the framework topic (`{namespace}-{event}`) to 256 characters (`AmazonSqsEntityNames.cs:26`), `Queue` fits the topology's queue name to 80 (`AmazonSqsEntityNames.cs:31`), and `ErrorQueue` appends `-error` before fitting to 80 (`AmazonSqsEntityNames.cs:36`).
- **Why it's built this way**: the limits are the SNS and SQS name caps, and fitting the error queue after the suffix is appended keeps it a legal name even for a queue name already at the cap.
- **Where it's used**: [`AmazonSqsPublisher`](#amazonsqspublisher) (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/Messaging/AmazonSqsPublisher.cs:34`) and [`AmazonSqsTopologyProvisioner`](#amazonsqstopologyprovisioner)`.ProvisionAsync` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/Messaging/AmazonSqsTopologyProvisioner.cs:96-97,115`); covered by `MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws.Tests/Messaging/AmazonSqsTopologyTests.cs`.

### AmazonSqsDeliveryHandler
> MMCA.Common.Aws · `MMCA.Common.Aws.Messaging` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/Messaging/AmazonSqsDeliveryHandler.cs:27` · Level 6 · class (internal sealed partial)

- **What it is**: hands one received SQS message to the framework's [`IBrokerReceivePipeline`](group-14-module-system-composition.md#ibrokerreceivepipeline) and carries out the [`BrokerDisposition`](group-14-module-system-composition.md#brokerdisposition) it returns; it decides nothing itself (`AmazonSqsDeliveryHandler.cs:8-9`).
- **Depends on**: [`IBrokerReceivePipeline`](group-14-module-system-composition.md#ibrokerreceivepipeline), [`BrokerDisposition`](group-14-module-system-composition.md#brokerdisposition), [`IAmazonSqsSettlement`](#iamazonsqssettlement), [`AmazonSqsMessageMapping`](#amazonsqsmessagemapping); BCL `TimeProvider`, `ILogger`; AWS SDK `Message`.
- **Concept**: the vendor-side half of the framework's receive pipeline: policy (retries, redelivery, dead-lettering) is decided once in the neutral pipeline, and each transport only translates the verdict into broker calls; see [`AzureServiceBusDeliveryHandler`](#azureservicebusdeliveryhandler) for the Azure translation. `[Rubric §10, Messaging & Integration]` assesses poison-message handling and delivery guarantees; here every disposition is "copy first, delete second", so a crash in between yields a duplicate the inbox absorbs rather than a lost message (`AmazonSqsDeliveryHandler.cs:12-15`). `[Rubric §29, Resilience & Business Continuity]` assesses graceful behavior under failure and shutdown; a visibility heartbeat and a best-effort release on stop keep a message owned by exactly one live consumer.
- **Walkthrough**: `VisibilityTimeoutSeconds = 60` (`AmazonSqsDeliveryHandler.cs:33`) and `HeartbeatInterval` at a third of it, 20 seconds, so two renewals can fail in a row before the message reappears (`AmazonSqsDeliveryHandler.cs:36`). The constructor null-checks the pipeline, clock and logger (`AmazonSqsDeliveryHandler.cs:46-55`). `HandleAsync` (`AmazonSqsDeliveryHandler.cs:62`) maps the headers, starts `RunHeartbeatAsync` on a linked token, awaits `ProcessAsync`, swallows an `OperationCanceledException` only when the host is stopping, then stops and awaits the heartbeat (`AmazonSqsDeliveryHandler.cs:67-86`). With no disposition (the host stopped) it calls `ReleaseBestEffortAsync`, which sets visibility to zero and only logs a failure (`AmazonSqsDeliveryHandler.cs:88-92,147-159`). `Completed` deletes (`AmazonSqsDeliveryHandler.cs:97-99`); `Redeliver` sends a copy to the queue with the redelivery count header and the delay in seconds, then deletes (`AmazonSqsDeliveryHandler.cs:101-108`); `DeadLetter` sends a copy to the error queue with the reason and detail headers, then deletes (`AmazonSqsDeliveryHandler.cs:110-116`); an unknown disposition throws, leaving the message to reappear (`AmazonSqsDeliveryHandler.cs:118-121`). Settlement calls pass `CancellationToken.None` (`AmazonSqsDeliveryHandler.cs:98,106`). `RunHeartbeatAsync` waits `HeartbeatInterval` on the injected `TimeProvider` and renews visibility to 60 seconds, logging and continuing on a failed renewal (`AmazonSqsDeliveryHandler.cs:125-145`). Two `[LoggerMessage]` warnings, EventId 10 (release failed) and 11 (heartbeat failed) (`AmazonSqsDeliveryHandler.cs:161,164`).
- **Why it's built this way**: a short 60-second visibility timeout means a message whose consumer died is redelivered within a minute, while the heartbeat keeps a slow but live message hidden (`AmazonSqsDeliveryHandler.cs:29-32`). Settling with no cancellation token is deliberate: the work is already done, and settling it during shutdown is what keeps it from running twice (`AmazonSqsDeliveryHandler.cs:23-25`). The second-level redelivery model is recorded in [ADR-087](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/087-broker-poison-message-handling.html).
- **Where it's used**: constructed by [`AmazonSqsTransport`](#amazonsqstransport)`.Register` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/Messaging/AmazonSqsTransport.cs:103-106`) and driven by [`AmazonSqsReceiver`](#amazonsqsreceiver) (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/Messaging/AmazonSqsReceiver.cs:195`); `VisibilityTimeoutSeconds` also sets the receive request and the queue attribute (`AmazonSqsReceiver.cs:92`, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/Messaging/AmazonSqsTopologyProvisioner.cs:189`); covered by `MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws.Tests/Messaging/AmazonSqsDeliveryHandlerTests.cs`.

### AmazonSqsMessageMapping
> MMCA.Common.Aws · `MMCA.Common.Aws.Messaging` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/Messaging/AmazonSqsMessageMapping.cs:19` · Level 6 · class (internal static)

- **What it is**: the pure translation between the framework's broker messages and SNS/SQS requests and messages, including how headers travel.
- **Depends on**: [`OutgoingBrokerMessage`](group-14-module-system-composition.md#outgoingbrokermessage), [`IncomingBrokerMessage`](group-14-module-system-composition.md#incomingbrokermessage), [`BrokerHeaders`](group-14-module-system-composition.md#brokerheaders), [`AmazonSqsTransport`](#amazonsqstransport) (for `MaxDelaySeconds`); BCL `Utf8JsonWriter`, `JsonDocument`; AWS SDK `PublishRequest`, `SendMessageRequest`, `Message`.
- **Concept**: header packing. SQS and SNS cap a message at 10 attributes, so every header travels as one JSON object in a single `mmca-headers` string attribute (`AmazonSqsMessageMapping.cs:22`; `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/Messaging/AmazonSqsTransport.cs:24-25`). The Azure sibling is [`AzureServiceBusMessageMapping`](#azureservicebusmessagemapping). `[Rubric §10, Messaging & Integration]` assesses wire-format robustness; malformed header text yields no headers, and the pipeline then dead-letters the message as an unknown event instead of guessing (`AmazonSqsMessageMapping.cs:57-60`).
