### NotificationHubConnection
> MMCA.Common.Azure · `MMCA.Common.Azure.Notifications` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Notifications/NotificationHubConnection.cs:15` · Level 0 · class (internal sealed partial)

- **What it is**: the parsed `NativePush` connection string and hub name, plus the Shared Access Signature (SAS) that authorizes every request the framework sends to Azure Notification Hubs (`NotificationHubConnection.cs:9-15`).
- **Depends on**: nothing first-party; BCL `HMACSHA256`, `HttpUtility.UrlEncode`, `UriBuilder` and a source-generated `Regex` (`:166-170`).
- **Concept introduced, a signed token per request instead of a vendor SDK.** `[Rubric §11, Security]` assesses how a service authenticates to the systems it calls and how long a credential it puts on the wire stays usable. Here the shared access key never leaves the process: each request carries a token signed with it over that request's own audience and valid for twenty minutes (`TokenTimeToLive`, `:21`). `[Rubric §32, Dependency & Supply-Chain]` assesses whether a third-party package earns its place. The class reproduces the parsing, the request URI shape and the token algorithm the retired Microsoft.Azure.NotificationHubs 4.2.0 SDK used, so the hub sees the same traffic without the package (`:9-14`).
- **Walkthrough**
  - `ApiVersion` is `"2020-06"` (`:18`); the key is held only as its UTF-8 bytes, set in the private constructor (`:27`, `:29-35`). `Endpoint`, `KeyName` and `HubName` are read-only (`:38`, `:41`, `:44`).
  - `Parse(connectionString, hubName)` (`:52-88`) splits `";" + connectionString` on `;Endpoint=`, `;SharedAccessKeyName=` and `;SharedAccessKey=`, case-insensitive and in any order, so a value runs to the next of the three keys (`:57-62`, regex at `:166-170`). An empty value, a repeated key or a missing key throws `FormatException` (`:65-85`).
  - `CreateRequestUriBuilder` (`:94-109`) forces `https`, appends `{hub}/` and the `api-version` query; `AddToQuery` (`:115-118`) appends a raw, unencoded fragment, which the registrations-by-tag paging relies on.
  - `CreateSharedAccessSignature(requestUri, utcNow)` (`:127-143`) normalizes the audience through `NormalizeAudience` (`:145-163`: `http` scheme, no port, query, fragment or credentials, trailing slash), lower-cases and URL-encodes it (`:130`), sets the expiry in Unix seconds twenty minutes out (`:132-134`), signs `{audience}\n{expiry}` with HMAC-SHA256 (`:137-140`) and returns `SharedAccessSignature sr=...&sig=...&se=...&skn=...` (`:142`).
- **Why it's built this way**: the clock is a parameter, so the client signs from its `TimeProvider` and a test can pin the token exactly. The lower-casing is deliberate (the CA1308 suppression at `:129` says the hub verifies over the lower-cased audience). [ADR-044](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/044-native-push-delivery.html) is the native push decision this serves.
- **Where it's used**: registered as a singleton factory by `AddAzureNotificationHubsPush` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:110`) and injected into [NotificationHubRestClient](#notificationhubrestclient), which signs every request with it (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Notifications/NotificationHubRestClient.cs:207`). Covered by `MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure.Tests/Notifications/NotificationHubConnectionTests.cs`.
- **Caveats / not-in-source**: the singleton is a factory registration, so a malformed connection string throws when the REST client is first resolved, not while the host builds its service collection.

### NotificationHubException
> MMCA.Common.Azure · `MMCA.Common.Azure.Notifications` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Notifications/NotificationHubContracts.cs:69` · Level 0 · class (internal sealed)

- **What it is**: the one failure type the hub client raises: a non-success status or a transport failure, classified as transient or permanent, with the hub's requested wait and a not-found flag (`NotificationHubContracts.cs:63-91`).
- **Depends on**: BCL `Exception` and `HttpStatusCode`.
- **Concept introduced, classify a failure once, where it is observed.** `[Rubric §29, Resilience & Business Continuity]` assesses whether a system tells a failure worth retrying from one that is not. The classification happens when the response is translated ([NotificationHubWireFormat](#notificationhubwireformat)), and everything downstream reads the flags instead of re-inspecting status codes: the retry policy reads `IsTransient` and `RetryAfter`, the registrar reads `IsEntityNotFound`.
- **Walkthrough**: the internal constructor takes the message, status, transient flag, optional wait and inner exception (`:72-78`). `StatusCode` is null for a transport failure (`:81`); `IsTransient` (`:84`); `RetryAfter` (`:87`); `IsEntityNotFound` is true for a 404 or a 204 where content was expected (`:90`). The pragma at `:68` suppresses the standard-constructor rules because the type is internal and only the client raises it.
- **Where it's used**: created by [NotificationHubWireFormat](#notificationhubwireformat) (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Notifications/NotificationHubWireFormat.cs:171-212`), classified by [NotificationHubRetryPolicy](#notificationhubretrypolicy), and caught by [AzureNotificationHubDeviceRegistrar](#azurenotificationhubdeviceregistrar) (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Notifications/AzureNotificationHubDeviceRegistrar.cs:69`, `:99`, `:104`, `:126`, `:204`). [AzureNotificationHubNativePushSender](#azurenotificationhubnativepushsender) catches any exception, this one included.

### NotificationHubRetryPolicy
> MMCA.Common.Azure · `MMCA.Common.Azure.Notifications` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Notifications/NotificationHubRetryPolicy.cs:14` · Level 1 · class (internal sealed)

- **What it is**: the hub client's retry: up to three retries of a transient failure with exponential backoff (2, 4, 8 seconds plus up to 8 percent jitter) capped at one minute, or the hub's own `Retry-After` wait when it sends one (`NotificationHubRetryPolicy.cs:6-14`).
- **Depends on**: [NotificationHubException](#notificationhubexception) for the classification; BCL `TimeProvider`, `ExceptionDispatchInfo`, `SocketException`, `TimeoutException`.
- **Concept introduced, retry what can succeed, wait as long as the server asks.** `[Rubric §29, Resilience & Business Continuity]` assesses whether a dependency's brownout is absorbed without amplifying it. The policy retries only failures classified transient, honors the hub's own wait, caps every wait, and never retries the caller's cancellation. The composition removes the standard resilience handlers from the typed client (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:121-132`), so this is the only retry layer and the two cannot multiply each other's attempts. `[Rubric §14, Testability]` applies through the `TimeProvider` parameter: the waits run on an injected clock.
- **Walkthrough**
  - `JitterFactor` is `0.08` (`:16`); the constructor takes the retry count, base delay and cap (`:18-23`); `Default` is 3 retries, one second, one minute (`:26`).
  - `RunAsync(operation, timeProvider, ct)` (`:40-67`) runs the operation, which builds a fresh request each time (new tracking id and token). On a failure it asks `CalculateRetryDelay`, then either waits through `Task.Delay(delay, timeProvider, ct)` (`:57`) or rethrows the original exception with its stack through `ExceptionDispatchInfo` (`:61`). A token cancelled between attempts ends the loop with `TaskCanceledException` (`:66`).
  - `CalculateRetryDelay(exception, attemptCount)` (`:70-88`) returns null when retries are disabled, exhausted or the failure is not retryable (`:72-79`); otherwise the hub's `RetryAfter`, or `2^n` times the base delay plus jitter (`:82-84`), capped at `MaxDelay` (`:87`).
  - `ShouldRetry` (`:95-110`) unwraps an `OperationCanceledException` to its inner exception (an attempt that hit the client timeout carries a `TimeoutException`, the caller's own cancellation carries none) and flattens an `AggregateException` (`:97-102`); a transient hub failure, a `TimeoutException` or a `SocketException` retries (`:104-109`).
- **Why it's built this way**: these are the defaults the Microsoft.Azure.NotificationHubs 4.2.0 SDK ran with, so replacing the package did not change how a brownout is weathered (`:10-12`).
- **Where it's used**: `Default` is passed to the client at registration (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:116`); [NotificationHubRestClient](#notificationhubrestclient) wraps every operation in it except the registrations-by-tag read. Covered by `MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure.Tests/Notifications/NotificationHubConnectionTests.cs`.

---

### INotificationHubRestClient
> MMCA.Common.Azure · `MMCA.Common.Azure.Notifications` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Notifications/NotificationHubRestClient.cs:14` · Level 2 · interface (internal)

- **What it is**: the five Azure Notification Hubs REST operations native push uses, with every failure surfacing as a [NotificationHubException](#notificationhubexception) (`NotificationHubRestClient.cs:8-30`).
- **Depends on**: [NotificationHubInstallation](#notificationhubinstallation), [NotificationHubInstallationState](#notificationhubinstallationstate), [NotificationHubRegistrationPage](#notificationhubregistrationpage), [NotificationHubNotification](#notificationhubnotification).
- **Concept**: `[Rubric §14, Testability]` assesses whether a component can be exercised without its external dependency. The sender and registrar take this interface, not an `HttpClient`, so their tests run against `MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure.Tests/Notifications/FakeNotificationHubRestClient.cs`, while the HTTP shape is pinned separately against the concrete client.
- **Walkthrough**: `CreateOrUpdateInstallationAsync` is `PUT installations/{id}`, a full overwrite (`:17`); `GetInstallationAsync` is `GET installations/{id}`, a 404 throwing a not-found failure (`:20`); `DeleteInstallationAsync` is `DELETE installations/{id}` (`:23`); `GetRegistrationsByTagAsync` reads one page of `GET tags/{tag}/registrations` (`:26`); `SendNotificationAsync` is `POST messages` with a tag expression (`:29`).
- **Where it's used**: implemented by [NotificationHubRestClient](#notificationhubrestclient) and registered as a typed `HttpClient` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:111`); consumed by [AzureNotificationHubNativePushSender](#azurenotificationhubnativepushsender) and [AzureNotificationHubDeviceRegistrar](#azurenotificationhubdeviceregistrar).

### NotificationHubWireFormat
> MMCA.Common.Azure · `MMCA.Common.Azure.Notifications` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Notifications/NotificationHubWireFormat.cs:18` · Level 2 · class (internal static partial)

- **What it is**: the bodies the hub exchanges: the installation JSON written on upsert and read back on get, the Atom feed of registrations, and the translation of a failed response into a [NotificationHubException](#notificationhubexception) (`NotificationHubWireFormat.cs:11-18`).
- **Depends on**: [NotificationHubInstallation](#notificationhubinstallation), [NotificationHubInstallationState](#notificationhubinstallationstate), [NotificationHubPlatform](#notificationhubplatform), [NotificationHubException](#notificationhubexception); BCL `JsonDocument`, `XmlReader` and source-generated `Regex`.
- **Concept introduced, replacing an SDK without changing a byte on the wire.** `[Rubric §32, Dependency & Supply-Chain]` assesses whether removing a dependency is done safely. Every shape here matches what the 4.2.0 SDK wrote and accepted, property order, null handling and string escaping included (`:14-16`), so the hub sees identical requests. `[Rubric §29, Resilience & Business Continuity]` applies through the failure translation, which is where transient and permanent are decided.
- **Walkthrough**
  - `WriteInstallation` (`:44-77`) hand-writes the JSON in the SDK's property order, with `userId`, `pushChannelExpired` and `expirationTime` as explicit nulls and `tags` only when set; the platform maps to `fcmV1` or `apns` and anything else throws (`:52-57`). `AppendString` (`:219-245`) escapes as the SDK's serializer did: quote, backslash and control characters (table built at `:248-264`) plus U+0085, U+2028 and U+2029.
  - `ReadInstallation` (`:84-118`) treats a blank body or JSON `null` as no installation, throws `JsonException` for a non-object, and reads `pushChannel` and `tags` case-insensitively (`:105-115`).
  - `ReadRegistrationTagsAsync` (`:139-163`) requires a `feed` root (`:145-148`) and reads each entry's `Tags` through `ReadDescriptionTagsAsync` (`:282-303`). `ParseTags` (`:306-320`) applies the hub's tag rules: a string with a malformed tag, more than one `$InstallationId` or `$UserId` system tag, or a tag over `MaximumTagSize` (120, `:24`) reads as no tags; the regexes are at `:349-357`.
  - `TranslateFailureAsync` (`:171-186`) builds a message with the status, the `<Error><Detail>` text (`ReadErrorDetail`, `:323-347`) and the `TrackingId`. A 429, or a 403 carrying `Retry-After`, is transient with the hub's wait or `DefaultThrottleWait` of ten seconds (`:34`, `:180-183`); otherwise only 500, 503, 504 and 408 are transient (`IsServerBusy`, `:189-193`).
  - `TranslateTransportFailure` (`:199-212`) marks a socket error transient unless it names an unreachable or refused host, and everything else permanent, with a null status.
- **Why it's built this way**: keeping every body in one static class lets `MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure.Tests/Notifications/NotificationHubRestClientRequestShapeTests.cs` pin the exact request shapes the SDK sent (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Notifications/NotificationHubRestClient.cs:32-39`).
- **Where it's used**: only by [NotificationHubRestClient](#notificationhubrestclient).

### AzureNotificationHubNativePushSender

> MMCA.Common.Azure · `MMCA.Common.Azure.Notifications` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Notifications/AzureNotificationHubNativePushSender.cs:25` · Level 3 · class (internal sealed partial)

- **What it is**: the Azure implementation of [INativePushSender](group-07-persistence-ef-core.md#inativepushsender), sending both the FCM v1 and the APNs payload for every notification to a set of users (resolved by tag) through the framework's own Notification Hubs REST client, and reporting the result as a [NativePushOutcome](group-07-persistence-ef-core.md#nativepushoutcome) counted in batches (`AzureNotificationHubNativePushSender.cs:7-27`).

- **Depends on**: [INotificationHubRestClient](#inotificationhubrestclient) (the hub's REST operations over `HttpClient`, with no Notification Hubs SDK) and `ILogger<T>` as its two primary-constructor parameters (`AzureNotificationHubNativePushSender.cs:25-27`), [NotificationHubNotification](#notificationhubnotification) for the two platform sends, [NativePushPayloads](group-14-module-system-composition.md#nativepushpayloads) for the payloads, [NativePushUserTags](#nativepushusertags) for the tag expressions, and [INativePushSender](group-07-persistence-ef-core.md#inativepushsender) as the contract.

- **Concept introduced, one logical notification, two platform sends.** `[Rubric §10, Messaging & Integration]` assesses whether a capability that varies by platform is normalized behind one application-facing call. A caller says "notify these users, with this title and body"; it does not know or care which of them are on iOS. This class turns that into two hub calls per audience chunk, one per platform, and the hub decides which installations each applies to. `[Rubric §29, Resilience & Business Continuity]` applies through the delivery posture in the class doc: every chunk is attempted on every platform, a failing send does not stop the later chunks or the other platform, and a failure is counted and returned rather than thrown (`:12-18`), which is the side-effect contract of [ADR-096](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/096-best-effort-side-effects.html). A push that fails does not fail the command that triggered it.

- **Walkthrough**
  - `SendToUsersAsync` (`AzureNotificationHubNativePushSender.cs:33-70`) builds each payload **once** (`:35-36`), then loops the tag expressions produced by [NativePushUserTags](#nativepushusertags) (`:43`), sending a `NotificationHubNotification.FcmV1` and a `NotificationHubNotification.Apple` per chunk (`:45`, `:56`) and counting each platform's sent and failed batches. For an audience of 45 users that is 3 chunks and 6 hub calls, and the payload serialization happened twice in total.
  - `TrySendAsync` (`:74-87`) wraps one send: success returns `null`; any exception other than the caller's own cancellation is logged at Warning with the platform and its type name (declared at `:92-93`) and returned, so it is counted and the loop continues (`:81-86`). The caller's cancellation propagates. A send reaches this catch only after [NotificationHubRetryPolicy](#notificationhubretrypolicy) inside the client has given up on it.
  - The outcome is `NativePushOutcome.From(NativePushDeliveryUnit.Batch, ...)` with the first failure attached (`:69`), after one source-generated Information log carrying the title and the per-platform batch counts (`:68`, declared at `:89-90`). The wording is precise: the notification was *handed to* the hub. Actual delivery to a device is asynchronous and outside this process.
  - Every await uses `ConfigureAwait(false)`, which is the library policy of [ADR-049](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/049-library-configureawait-policy.html).

- **Why it's built this way**: sending the two payloads unconditionally rather than looking up each user's platform keeps the sender free of any device registry of its own. The hub already knows each installation's platform from the registration written by [AzureNotificationHubDeviceRegistrar](#azurenotificationhubdeviceregistrar), so a payload that does not apply to a given installation is simply not delivered to it. That is the division of labour [ADR-044](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/044-native-push-delivery.html) records: the hub is the device registry, and this codebase stores no push tokens. [NotificationHubRestClient](#notificationhubrestclient) issues the same requests the Microsoft.Azure.NotificationHubs 4.2.0 SDK did, request for request and with its retry policy (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Notifications/NotificationHubRestClient.cs:32-39`), so the HTTP traffic is unchanged. [ADR-134](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/134-cloud-neutral-core-vendor-adapters.html) records why it lives in the vendor adapter assembly rather than the core.

- **Where it's used**: registered only by [AzureServiceCollectionExtensions](#azureservicecollectionextensions)`.AddAzureNotificationHubsPush` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:133`), and only when [AzureNotificationHubSettings](#azurenotificationhubsettings) has `Enabled: true`, a connection string and a hub name (`:102-107`); the REST client is registered as a typed `HttpClient` in the same call, with its factory loggers and standard resilience handlers removed (`:110-132`). The type is internal, so a host resolves `INativePushSender`, never this class. The consuming application code is [SendPushNotificationHandler](group-10-notifications.md#sendpushnotificationhandler).

- **Caveats / not-in-source**: whether a given deployment has working platform credentials is a provisioning question, Not determinable from source.

### NotificationHubRestClient
> MMCA.Common.Azure · `MMCA.Common.Azure.Notifications` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Notifications/NotificationHubRestClient.cs:40` · Level 3 · class (internal sealed)

- **What it is**: the [INotificationHubRestClient](#inotificationhubrestclient) implementation over a factory `HttpClient`, authorized with a Shared Access Signature per request, whose request shapes, accepted statuses, failure classification and retry are those of the retired Microsoft.Azure.NotificationHubs 4.2.0 SDK (`NotificationHubRestClient.cs:32-40`).
- **Depends on**: [NotificationHubConnection](#notificationhubconnection) for the URIs and tokens, [NotificationHubRetryPolicy](#notificationhubretrypolicy), [NotificationHubWireFormat](#notificationhubwireformat) for bodies and failures, and the contract records ([NotificationHubInstallation](#notificationhubinstallation), [NotificationHubInstallationState](#notificationhubinstallationstate), [NotificationHubRegistrationPage](#notificationhubregistrationpage), [NotificationHubNotification](#notificationhubnotification)); BCL `HttpClient` and `TimeProvider`.
- **Concept introduced, owning the vendor protocol in a few hundred lines.** `[Rubric §32, Dependency & Supply-Chain]` assesses whether a dependency is worth what it costs. Native push needs five REST calls, so the framework issues them itself rather than carrying an SDK, and pins the traffic with request-shape tests so the swap is observable. `[Rubric §13, Observability & Operability]` applies through the per-request `TrackingId` header, which every failure message repeats so a hub-side trace can be matched, and through the composition's decision to remove the factory loggers because the hub's URIs carry user tags and installation ids (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:117-120`).
- **Walkthrough**
  - `OperationTimeout` is 60 seconds per attempt (`:43`); `UserAgent` is the SDK's `NHub/2020-06 (api-origin=DotNetSdk;...)` string (`:46-47`); the accepted statuses are 200, 204, or 200 and 201 (`:51-53`). The constructor sets the timeout and adds the `User-Agent` on the fresh factory client (`:64-76`).
  - `CreateOrUpdateInstallationAsync` (`:79-101`) rejects a blank id with `InvalidOperationException` (`:82-85`) and `PUT`s the JSON from `WriteInstallation`, accepting only 200. `GetInstallationAsync` (`:104-121`) reads it back through `ReadInstallation`; `DeleteInstallationAsync` (`:124-140`) accepts only 204.
  - `GetRegistrationsByTagAsync` (`:143-171`) appends `$top` and the continuation token as raw query fragments (`:149-158`) and builds the page from the Atom feed and the `x-ms-continuationtoken` header (`:165-169`). It is the one operation sent once, without the retry policy, as the SDK did (`:37-38`).
  - `SendNotificationAsync` (`:174-199`) `POST`s to `messages` with `ServiceBusNotification-Tags` when a tag expression is given (`:185-188`) and `ServiceBusNotification-Format` from the notification (`:190`), accepting 200 or 201.
  - `CreateRequest` (`:201-209`) gives every attempt a new GUID `TrackingId` and a freshly signed `Authorization` header from the injected clock. `SendAsync` (`:211-232`) maps an `HttpRequestException` through `TranslateTransportFailure` (`:218-221`) and any unaccepted status through `TranslateFailureAsync` (`:228-231`).
- **Why it's built this way**: the retry lives in [NotificationHubRetryPolicy](#notificationhubretrypolicy) alone because the composition strips the standard resilience handlers from this typed client (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:121-132`), so attempts are not multiplied by two stacked policies. [ADR-044](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/044-native-push-delivery.html) and [ADR-134](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/134-cloud-neutral-core-vendor-adapters.html) place it in the Azure adapter assembly.
- **Where it's used**: registered by `AddHttpClient<INotificationHubRestClient, NotificationHubRestClient>` with `TimeProvider` from DI or `TimeProvider.System` and `NotificationHubRetryPolicy.Default` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:111-116`); reached through the interface by [AzureNotificationHubNativePushSender](#azurenotificationhubnativepushsender) and [AzureNotificationHubDeviceRegistrar](#azurenotificationhubdeviceregistrar). Covered by `MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure.Tests/Notifications/NotificationHubRestClientRequestShapeTests.cs`.

### AzureNotificationHubDeviceRegistrar

> MMCA.Common.Azure · `MMCA.Common.Azure.Notifications` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Notifications/AzureNotificationHubDeviceRegistrar.cs:15` · Level 4 · class (internal sealed)

- **What it is**: the Azure implementation of [IPushDeviceRegistrar](group-07-persistence-ef-core.md#ipushdeviceregistrar). It maintains device installations in Azure Notification Hubs using the installation model (client-generated stable ids, full upsert semantics) and stamps each installation with its owner's `user:{id}` tag so sends can target users rather than raw device tokens (`AzureNotificationHubDeviceRegistrar.cs:8-225`).

- **Depends on**: [INotificationHubRestClient](#inotificationhubrestclient) (whose failures all surface as [NotificationHubException](#notificationhubexception), not-found flagged by `IsEntityNotFound`) and `ILogger<T>` as its two primary-constructor parameters (`AzureNotificationHubDeviceRegistrar.cs:15-17`), [NotificationHubInstallation](#notificationhubinstallation), [NotificationHubInstallationState](#notificationhubinstallationstate) and [NotificationHubPlatform](#notificationhubplatform) as the hub shapes, [NativePushUserTags](#nativepushusertags) for the owner tag, [DeviceInstallationRequest](group-10-notifications.md#deviceinstallationrequest) as the input shape, and [Result](group-01-result-error-handling.md#result) / [Error](group-01-result-error-handling.md#error).

- **Concept introduced, the hub as the only ownership store, and the existence oracle it must not become.** `[Rubric §11, Security]` assesses whether an endpoint can be used to learn something the caller is not entitled to know, and `[Rubric §9, API & Contract Design]` assesses whether the result shape is honest about what happened. Installation ids are client-generated, so nothing stops a caller from sending someone else's, and the upsert is a full overwrite that would re-tag and re-point another user's device. The framework stores no device table of its own, so both checks run against the hub: the delete is a read-then-delete comparing the owner tag (`AzureNotificationHubDeviceRegistrar.cs:88-96`), and the upsert first asks `ClaimedByAnotherDeviceAsync` (`:197-215`). The installation id is per device, so a second user signing in on the same phone legitimately presents an id the previous user owns; when the request also carries the installation's current push channel the caller holds the device and the installation is handed over (re-tagged, so the previous owner's pushes stop). Without the matching channel the upsert answers with a conflict, which reveals nothing exploitable because ids are random client GUIDs, and the client recovers by minting a fresh id (`:50-64`). The delete path reports a mismatch as success, not as not-found; the interface remarks explain why (`MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Application/Interfaces/Infrastructure/Notifications/IPushDeviceRegistrar.cs:46`): answering differently for "no such installation" and "not yours" would turn the endpoint into an existence oracle for other users' installation ids.

- **Walkthrough**
  - Two constants frame the class: `InstallationIdTagPrefix` is `"$InstallationId:{"` (`AzureNotificationHubDeviceRegistrar.cs:20`) and `RegistrationPageSize` is 100, the hub's page cap (`:23`).
  - `UpsertAsync(userId, request, ct)` (`:26-77`) starts with the platform switch: `FCMV1` and `APNS`, compared after `ToUpperInvariant`, map to `NotificationHubPlatform.FcmV1` and `.Apns`, and anything else yields `null` (`:28-33`).
  - An unmapped platform returns a validation failure with code `PushDevice.UnsupportedPlatform`, and the message quotes the two accepted constants from [DeviceInstallationRequest](group-10-notifications.md#deviceinstallationrequest) (`:34-40`).
  - The `NotificationHubInstallation` is assembled with the client-supplied id, the resolved platform, the push channel, and exactly one tag, the owner stamp from `NativePushUserTags.UserTag` (`:42-46`). Before writing, the upsert calls `ClaimedByAnotherDeviceAsync`; when it returns true the result is `Error.Conflict` with code `PushDevice.InstallationConflict` (`:58-64`). `CreateOrUpdateInstallationAsync` is a full upsert, so re-registering the same id replaces the record rather than accumulating duplicates (`:66`).
  - `ClaimedByAnotherDeviceAsync` (`:197-215`) is true only when all three hold: the installation exists (a not-found `NotificationHubException` on the read means free, `:204-207`), it carries a `user:`-prefixed tag that is not the caller's own (`:209-212`), and its stored push channel differs from the request's, compared ordinally (`:213-214`).
  - The upsert's catch is narrow (`NotificationHubException`) and translates to `PushDevice.UpsertFailed` after logging the real exception (`:69-76`).
  - `DeleteAsync(userId, installationId, ct)` (`:80-109`) reads the installation, checks ownership, and deletes only on a match (`:88-96`). The comment at `:84-87` settles the concurrency question: a concurrent re-registration of the same id between the read and the delete is the owner's own doing, so no lock is warranted.
  - Two catches follow. A not-found `NotificationHubException` returns success, because an unknown installation is already the desired state (`:99-103`); any other `NotificationHubException` logs and returns `PushDevice.DeleteFailed` (`:104-108`).
  - `DeleteAllForUserAsync` (`:119-143`) finds the user's installations through the registrations the hub materializes for them: it pages the registrations tagged with the owner tag (`FindInstallationIdsAsync`, `:150-173`), reads each one's `$InstallationId:{id}` system tag (`TryReadInstallationId`, `:176-189`), and sends every id through `DeleteAsync`, which re-checks ownership (remarks at `:112-118`).
  - `OwnedBy` (`:217-218`) checks the tags contain the owner tag with `StringComparer.Ordinal`. `DeleteFailed` (`:220-224`) is factored out so the failure shape is written once.

- **Why it's built this way**: [ADR-044](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/044-native-push-delivery.html) puts the device registry in the hub rather than in the application database, which keeps push tokens (a credential-shaped secret) out of the application's storage entirely. The cost of that choice is the read-then-delete ownership check here, and the interface remarks accept it explicitly.

- **Where it's used**: registered by `AddAzureNotificationHubsPush` alongside the sender (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:134`); the type is internal, so a host resolves `IPushDeviceRegistrar`. Called from the framework's [DevicesController](group-10-notifications.md#devicescontroller), which lets a client register a device without any application-specific endpoint. The AWS and GCP siblings are [SnsPushDeviceRegistrar](#snspushdeviceregistrar) and [FcmPushDeviceRegistrar](#fcmpushdeviceregistrar). Covered by `MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure.Tests/Notifications/AzureNotificationHubDeviceRegistrarTests.cs`.

- **Caveats / not-in-source**: an installation registered before ownership tagging carries no `user:` tag, and the delete path treats it exactly like someone else's (success, no delete), as the comment at `:91-92` says. On the upsert side such an installation is claimable by any caller, because it has no other user's owner tag. Nothing in this code cleans up such records.

### AzureBlobFileStorageSettings
> MMCA.Common.Azure · `MMCA.Common.Azure.Storage` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Storage/AzureBlobFileStorageSettings.cs:10` · Level 0 · class (public sealed)

- **What it is**: the options class bound from the `FileStorage` section for the Azure Blob adapter: a blob service endpoint, a connection-string alternative, and the one container every blob lives in (`AzureBlobFileStorageSettings.cs:10-23`).
- **Depends on**: nothing first-party; `System.Uri` only.
- **Concept**: a plain bound-options class; the fail-fast configuration contract it participates in is recorded in [ADR-070](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/070-fail-fast-configuration-contract.html). `[Rubric §11, Security]` assesses whether production authenticates with an identity rather than a shared key: `ServiceUri` is the managed-identity path (`DefaultAzureCredential`), and `ConnectionString` is documented as the local-development (Azurite) alternative (`:15-19`).
- **Walkthrough**: `SectionName` is `"FileStorage"` (`:13`); `ServiceUri` (`:16`), `ConnectionString` (`:19`) and `ContainerName` (`:22`, documented as required) are `init`-only.
- **Why it's built this way**: one section with two mutually-exclusive credential shapes lets the same host code run against Azurite locally and a managed identity in Azure. It shares the `FileStorage` section name with [S3FileStorageSettings](#s3filestoragesettings), so a host picks its vendor by which registration it calls, not by renaming configuration ([ADR-045](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/045-managed-file-storage-and-avatars.html)).
- **Where it's used**: [AzureServiceCollectionExtensions](#azureservicecollectionextensions)`.AddAzureBlobFileStorage` binds it (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:48-51`) and registers nothing when `ContainerName` is blank or neither an absolute `ServiceUri` nor a `ConnectionString` is present (`:49-59`); the comment at `:54` records the trap that an empty-string `ServiceUri` binds to a relative `Uri`, so only `IsAbsoluteUri` counts.

### AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationSettings
> MMCA.Common.Azure · `MMCA.Common.Azure.PostgreSql` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/PostgreSql/AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationSettings.cs:10` · Level 0 · class (internal sealed)

- **What it is**: the bound options for Microsoft Entra token login to Azure Database for PostgreSQL, section `AzurePostgreSql:EntraAuthentication` (`AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationSettings.cs:13`).
- **Depends on**: nothing first-party.
- **Concept**: cross-reference [`AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator`](#azurepostgresqlentraauthenticationconfigurator), which introduces the Azure form of token-as-password database authentication.
- **Walkthrough**: a single member, `Enabled` (`AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationSettings.cs:16`), off when absent. The class doc records why it needs nothing else: the database user is the connection string's `Username` (the Entra principal name), and the identity whose token is sent is whatever `DefaultAzureCredential` resolves to, a user-assigned managed identity being selected through `AZURE_CLIENT_ID` (`AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationSettings.cs:3-9`). It mirrors the GCP sibling [`CloudSqlIamAuthenticationSettings`](#cloudsqliamauthenticationsettings).
- **Why it's built this way**: an explicit boolean gate switches PostgreSQL onto Entra tokens by configuration alone, and an absent or false value makes registration a no-op (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:185-191`). See [ADR-070](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/070-fail-fast-configuration-contract.html) and [ADR-134](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/134-cloud-neutral-core-vendor-adapters.html).
- **Where it's used**: bound in `AddAzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthentication` of [`AzureServiceCollectionExtensions`](#azureservicecollectionextensions) (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:186-187`); `SectionPath` is quoted in the TLS error of [`AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator`](#azurepostgresqlentraauthenticationconfigurator) (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/PostgreSql/AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:103`).

### AzureRedisEntraAuthenticationSettings
> MMCA.Common.Azure · `MMCA.Common.Azure.Redis` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Redis/AzureRedisEntraAuthenticationSettings.cs:10` · Level 0 · class (internal sealed)

- **What it is**: the bound options for Microsoft Entra token login to Azure Cache for Redis and Azure Managed Redis, section `AzureRedis:EntraAuthentication` (`AzureRedisEntraAuthenticationSettings.cs:13`).
- **Depends on**: nothing first-party.
- **Concept**: cross-reference [`AzureRedisEntraAuthenticationConfigurator`](#azureredisentraauthenticationconfigurator), which introduces token login on a Redis connection.
- **Walkthrough**: `Enabled` (`AzureRedisEntraAuthenticationSettings.cs:16`) switches every framework Redis connection onto Entra tokens and is off when absent. `ManagedIdentityClientId` (`AzureRedisEntraAuthenticationSettings.cs:22`) names the user-assigned managed identity to sign in as; blank selects the system-assigned identity, or the one `AZURE_CLIENT_ID` names (`AzureRedisEntraAuthenticationSettings.cs:18-21`). The connection string keeps host, port and TLS settings and carries no access key (`AzureRedisEntraAuthenticationSettings.cs:3-9`).
- **Why it's built this way**: unlike [`AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationSettings`](#azurepostgresqlentraauthenticationsettings), it carries the identity explicitly, and registration hands it to [`AzureTokenCredentials`](#azuretokencredentials)`.Create` so the credential is pinned to that identity (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:253`). An absent or false `Enabled` registers nothing (`AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:234-240`), per [ADR-070](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/070-fail-fast-configuration-contract.html).
- **Where it's used**: bound in `AddAzureRedisEntraAuthentication` of [`AzureServiceCollectionExtensions`](#azureservicecollectionextensions) (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:235-236`); `SectionPath` is quoted in both failure messages of [`AzureRedisEntraAuthenticationConfigurator`](#azureredisentraauthenticationconfigurator) (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Redis/AzureRedisEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:48,59`).

### CloudSqlIamAuthenticationSettings
> MMCA.Common.Gcp · `MMCA.Common.Gcp.CloudSql` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/CloudSql/CloudSqlIamAuthenticationSettings.cs:10` · Level 0 · class (internal sealed)

- **What it is**: the bound options for Cloud SQL IAM database authentication, section `CloudSql:IamAuthentication` (`CloudSqlIamAuthenticationSettings.cs:13`).
- **Depends on**: nothing first-party.
- **Concept**: cross-reference [`CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator`](#cloudsqliamauthenticationconfigurator), which introduces the GCP token-as-password flow.
- **Walkthrough**: a single member, `Enabled` (`CloudSqlIamAuthenticationSettings.cs:16`), off when absent. Unlike the AWS sibling [`RdsIamAuthenticationSettings`](#rdsiamauthenticationsettings) it carries no region or credential keys, because the token comes from Application Default Credentials ([`CloudSqlAccessTokenSource`](#cloudsqlaccesstokensource)).
- **Why it's built this way**: an explicit boolean gate lets a deployment switch PostgreSQL onto IAM tokens by configuration alone, and an absent or false value makes registration a no-op (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/GcpServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:96-99`). See [ADR-070](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/070-fail-fast-configuration-contract.html), [ADR-113](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/113-postgresql-as-a-first-class-engine.html) and [ADR-134](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/134-cloud-neutral-core-vendor-adapters.html).
- **Where it's used**: bound in `AddCloudSqlIamAuthentication` of [`GcpServiceCollectionExtensions`](#gcpservicecollectionextensions) (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/GcpServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:95`); `SectionPath` is quoted in the TLS error of [`CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator`](#cloudsqliamauthenticationconfigurator) (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/CloudSql/CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:106`).

### GcpKeyRingAtRestNoticeService
> MMCA.Common.Gcp · `MMCA.Common.Gcp.DataProtection` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/DataProtection/GcpKeyRingAtRestNoticeService.cs:14` · Level 0 · class (internal sealed partial)

- **What it is**: an `IHostedService` that logs one startup Warning when the shared DataProtection key ring is persisted to Cloud Storage with no `DataProtection:GcpKmsKeyName`, in a host that is not Development, because the key ring XML then sits in the object without an at-rest key (`GcpKeyRingAtRestNoticeService.cs:6-11`).
- **Depends on**: nothing first-party; `ILogger<T>` as its one primary-constructor parameter and `IHostedService` (`GcpKeyRingAtRestNoticeService.cs:14-15`).
- **Concept**: cross-reference [`GcpKmsXmlEncryptor`](#gcpkmsxmlencryptor), which introduces key-ring encryption at rest; this type is the visible signal when that encryptor is not configured. The Azure sibling is [`AzureKeyRingAtRestNoticeService`](#azurekeyringatrestnoticeservice).
- **Walkthrough**: `KmsKeyNameKey` is `"DataProtection:GcpKmsKeyName"` (`GcpKeyRingAtRestNoticeService.cs:18`). `StartAsync` logs the notice once and completes (`GcpKeyRingAtRestNoticeService.cs:21-25`); `StopAsync` does nothing (`GcpKeyRingAtRestNoticeService.cs:28`). The message is a source-generated `[LoggerMessage]` at `LogLevel.Warning` that names the configuration key to set (`GcpKeyRingAtRestNoticeService.cs:30-33`).
- **Why it's built this way**: a missing KMS key is a warning, not a startup failure, for the reason the Azure sibling gives (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/GoogleCloudDataProtectionExtensions.cs:118-119`). It is registered only in the case it reports, so a host that configured the key, or runs in Development, logs nothing (`GcpKeyRingAtRestNoticeService.cs:9-11`). The key constant lives here and the registration reuses it, so the notice and the setting cannot name different keys (`GoogleCloudDataProtectionExtensions.cs:25`). See [ADR-069](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/069-shared-data-protection-key-ring.html).
- **Where it's used**: added with `AddHostedService` by [`GoogleCloudDataProtectionExtensions`](#googleclouddataprotectionextensions)`.AddGoogleCloudDataProtection` when no KMS key name was parsed and the environment is not Development (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/GoogleCloudDataProtectionExtensions.cs:112-121`).

### GcpKmsXmlDecryptor
> MMCA.Common.Gcp · `MMCA.Common.Gcp.DataProtection` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/DataProtection/GcpKmsXmlDecryptor.cs:16` · Level 0 · class (internal sealed)

- **What it is**: the ASP.NET Core DataProtection `IXmlDecryptor` that reverses [`GcpKmsXmlEncryptor`](#gcpkmsxmlencryptor), decrypting a stored key element with the Cloud KMS key that element names (`GcpKmsXmlDecryptor.cs:10-15`).
- **Depends on**: [`GcpKmsXmlEncryptor`](#gcpkmsxmlencryptor) for the element-name constants; Google `KeyManagementServiceClient`, `DecryptRequest`, `ByteString`; `IServiceProvider`.
- **Concept**: cross-reference [`GcpKmsXmlEncryptor`](#gcpkmsxmlencryptor) for the envelope format. The decryptor half of a DataProtection pair is activated by type: the encryptor records the decryptor's type in the key ring, and DataProtection later instantiates it through an `IServiceProvider` constructor (`GcpKmsXmlDecryptor.cs:12-14`).
- **Walkthrough**: `_client` (`GcpKmsXmlDecryptor.cs:18`) is resolved in the `IServiceProvider` constructor with `GetRequiredService<KeyManagementServiceClient>()` (`GcpKmsXmlDecryptor.cs:22-25`). `Decrypt(encryptedElement)` (`GcpKmsXmlDecryptor.cs:29`) reads the `kmsKeyName` and `value` children (`GcpKmsXmlDecryptor.cs:33-34`) and throws `InvalidOperationException` naming both elements when either is blank (`GcpKmsXmlDecryptor.cs:35-39`). It then sends a synchronous `DecryptRequest` with the named key and the base64-decoded ciphertext (`GcpKmsXmlDecryptor.cs:41-45`) and parses the UTF-8 plaintext back into an `XElement` (`GcpKmsXmlDecryptor.cs:47`).
- **Why it's built this way**: the key name is read from the element rather than from configuration, so a ring encrypted under one key keeps decrypting regardless of current settings. Taking the client from the container means it is the one `AddGoogleCloudDataProtection` registers or one the host registered first (`GcpKmsXmlDecryptor.cs:14-15`); see [`GcpDataProtectionBuilderExtensions`](#gcpdataprotectionbuilderextensions).
- **Where it's used**: named as the decryptor type in `GcpKmsXmlEncryptor.Encrypt` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/DataProtection/GcpKmsXmlEncryptor.cs:68`); exercised by `MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp.Tests/DataProtection/GcpKmsXmlEncryptionTests.cs`.

### GcpKmsXmlEncryptor
> MMCA.Common.Gcp · `MMCA.Common.Gcp.DataProtection` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/DataProtection/GcpKmsXmlEncryptor.cs:25` · Level 0 · class (internal sealed)

- **What it is**: the DataProtection `IXmlEncryptor` that encrypts each key element with a Cloud KMS symmetric key before it reaches the key ring, so the stored object holds only ciphertext (`GcpKmsXmlEncryptor.cs:9-13`).
- **Depends on**: [`GcpKmsXmlDecryptor`](#gcpkmsxmldecryptor) (named in the result); Google `KeyManagementServiceClient`, `CryptoKeyName`, `EncryptRequest`, `ByteString`; DataProtection `EncryptedXmlInfo`.
- **Concept introduced, key-ring encryption at rest with a cloud KMS.** DataProtection lets a host plug an `IXmlEncryptor` between key generation and the repository; the encryptor returns the stored form plus the decryptor type that can reverse it. `[Rubric §11, Security]` assesses protection of secrets at rest; here reading a key needs `cloudkms.cryptoKeyVersions.useToDecrypt` on top of read access to the bucket (`GcpKmsXmlEncryptor.cs:11-12`), so a leaked bucket alone does not leak the keys.
- **Walkthrough**: three element-name constants, `encryptedKey`, `kmsKeyName` and `value` (`GcpKmsXmlEncryptor.cs:28,31,34`). The internal constructor takes the client and a `CryptoKeyName`, null-checking both (`GcpKmsXmlEncryptor.cs:41-45`); `KeyName` exposes the key (`GcpKmsXmlEncryptor.cs:48`). `Encrypt(plaintextElement)` (`GcpKmsXmlEncryptor.cs:51`) serializes the element with `SaveOptions.DisableFormatting` to UTF-8 (`GcpKmsXmlEncryptor.cs:55`), sends a synchronous `EncryptRequest` (`GcpKmsXmlEncryptor.cs:56-60`), builds `<encryptedKey>` with an explanatory comment, the key name and the base64 ciphertext (`GcpKmsXmlEncryptor.cs:62-66`), and returns `EncryptedXmlInfo(encrypted, typeof(GcpKmsXmlDecryptor))` (`GcpKmsXmlEncryptor.cs:68`).
- **Why it's built this way**: the element is sent to KMS whole because a key element is about a kilobyte, far under the 64 KiB plaintext limit (`GcpKmsXmlEncryptor.cs:12-13`), so no local envelope key is needed. The ciphertext records the key version, so rotating the KMS key needs no code change and old keys keep decrypting while their version is enabled (`GcpKmsXmlEncryptor.cs:21-22`).
- **Where it's used**: constructed by `ProtectKeysWithGoogleCloudKms` in [`GcpDataProtectionBuilderExtensions`](#gcpdataprotectionbuilderextensions) (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/DataProtection/GcpDataProtectionBuilderExtensions.cs:59`); its constants are read by [`GcpKmsXmlDecryptor`](#gcpkmsxmldecryptor); exercised by `MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp.Tests/DataProtection/GcpKmsXmlEncryptionTests.cs` and `MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp.Tests/GoogleCloudDataProtectionExtensionsTests.cs`.

### GcsObjectLocation
> MMCA.Common.Gcp · `MMCA.Common.Gcp.DataProtection` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/DataProtection/GcsObjectLocation.cs:11` · Level 0 · record (internal sealed)

- **What it is**: the Cloud Storage object that holds the DataProtection key ring, a positional record of `Bucket` and `ObjectName` parsed from the `gs://bucket/object` form of `DataProtection:GcsObjectUri` (`GcsObjectLocation.cs:3-11`).
- **Depends on**: nothing first-party.
- **Concept**: a small parse-once value that fails loudly on a malformed setting, the fail-fast configuration contract of [ADR-070](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/070-fail-fast-configuration-contract.html).
- **Walkthrough**: `Scheme` is `gs://` (`GcsObjectLocation.cs:13`). `Parse(value, key)` (`GcsObjectLocation.cs:23`) matches the scheme case-insensitively (`GcsObjectLocation.cs:27`), splits the remainder at the first `/` (`GcsObjectLocation.cs:29-30`), and accepts it only when the bucket is non-empty, the object name is non-empty and the value does not end in `/` (`GcsObjectLocation.cs:31-33`); anything else throws `InvalidOperationException` naming the configuration key and the bad value (`GcsObjectLocation.cs:37-38`). `ToString()` reassembles `gs://bucket/object` (`GcsObjectLocation.cs:42`).
- **Why it's built this way**: parsed by hand rather than through `Uri`, which lower-cases and re-escapes an unknown scheme's authority and path in ways a bucket or object name must not be (`GcsObjectLocation.cs:5-7`). A trailing `/` is rejected because it names a folder, not an object (`GcsObjectLocation.cs:16-17`).
- **Where it's used**: parsed in [`GoogleCloudDataProtectionExtensions`](#googleclouddataprotectionextensions) before anything is registered (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/GoogleCloudDataProtectionExtensions.cs:91`), passed through `PersistKeysToGoogleCloudStorage` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/DataProtection/GcpDataProtectionBuilderExtensions.cs:34`) into [`GcsXmlRepository`](#gcsxmlrepository); exercised by `MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp.Tests/DataProtection/GcsObjectLocationTests.cs`.

### AzurePostgreSqlAccessTokenSource
> MMCA.Common.Azure · `MMCA.Common.Azure.PostgreSql` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/PostgreSql/AzurePostgreSqlAccessTokenSource.cs:12` · Level 1 · class (internal sealed)

- **What it is**: the default token source behind [`AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator`](#azurepostgresqlentraauthenticationconfigurator): it returns a Microsoft Entra access token for the Azure Database for PostgreSQL scope (`AzurePostgreSqlAccessTokenSource.cs:5-10`).
- **Depends on**: nothing first-party; `Azure.Core.TokenCredential` and `TokenRequestContext`.
- **Concept**: cross-reference [`AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator`](#azurepostgresqlentraauthenticationconfigurator) for token-as-password; the GCP counterpart is [`CloudSqlAccessTokenSource`](#cloudsqlaccesstokensource).
- **Walkthrough**: `Scope` is `https://ossrdbms-aad.database.windows.net/.default` (`AzurePostgreSqlAccessTokenSource.cs:15`). The primary constructor takes a `TokenCredential`, null-checked into `_credential` (`AzurePostgreSqlAccessTokenSource.cs:12,17`). `GetTokenAsync(cancellationToken)` (`AzurePostgreSqlAccessTokenSource.cs:22`) requests a token for that one scope and returns its string, sent to the server as the password (`AzurePostgreSqlAccessTokenSource.cs:24-27`).
- **Why it's built this way**: the credential is created with the configurator but no token is requested until the first connection, and the credential caches and refreshes its own token, so each call is an in-memory read until the token nears expiry (`AzurePostgreSqlAccessTokenSource.cs:7-9`). Unlike the GCP sibling it needs no lazy-load field, because the credential object is passed in rather than loaded. See [ADR-134](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/134-cloud-neutral-core-vendor-adapters.html).
- **Where it's used**: the public constructor of [`AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator`](#azurepostgresqlentraauthenticationconfigurator) builds it over `new DefaultAzureCredential()` and binds `GetTokenAsync` as the token delegate (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/PostgreSql/AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:34-35`); referenced by `MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure.Tests/PostgreSql/AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationTests.cs`.

### AzureRedisEntraAuthenticationConfigurator
> MMCA.Common.Azure · `MMCA.Common.Azure.Redis` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Redis/AzureRedisEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:21` · Level 1 · class (internal sealed)

- **What it is**: an [`IRedisConfigurationOptionsConfigurator`](group-09-caching.md#iredisconfigurationoptionsconfigurator) that puts every framework Redis connection on Microsoft Entra token login through Microsoft.Azure.StackExchangeRedis: the user and password become the identity's object id and an Entra access token, and the library re-authenticates each open connection before the current token expires (`AzureRedisEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:7-13`).
- **Depends on**: [`IRedisConfigurationOptionsConfigurator`](group-09-caching.md#iredisconfigurationoptionsconfigurator), [`AzureRedisEntraAuthenticationSettings`](#azureredisentraauthenticationsettings) (for `SectionPath` in its messages); externals `Azure.Core.TokenCredential`, StackExchange.Redis `ConfigurationOptions` and `EndPointCollection`, and the library's `ConfigureForAzureWithTokenCredentialAsync`.
- **Concept introduced, bearer-token login on a cache connection.** The token-as-password pattern of [`AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator`](#azurepostgresqlentraauthenticationconfigurator) applied to Redis, where the access key would otherwise be the shared secret. `[Rubric §11, Security]` assesses credential handling: no access key is needed, and because the token is a bearer credential sent as the password, a connection without TLS or one that still carries an access key is rejected rather than connected (`AzureRedisEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:11-13`). `[Rubric §14, Testability]` also applies: an internal constructor takes the configuration step as a delegate.
- **Walkthrough**: the public constructor takes a `TokenCredential` and chains to `CreateConfigureStep` (`AzureRedisEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:27-28`), which null-checks it and returns `options => options.ConfigureForAzureWithTokenCredentialAsync(credential)` (`AzureRedisEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:64-67`); the internal constructor accepts any `Func<ConfigurationOptions, Task>` for tests (`AzureRedisEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:34-35`). `Configure(options)` (`AzureRedisEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:41`) first throws when the connection string carries a password, because an explicit password takes precedence over the token and the connection would keep signing in with the access key (`AzureRedisEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:45-50`). It then runs the library step synchronously under a `VSTHRD002` suppression (`AzureRedisEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:52-54`), and afterwards throws when `options.Ssl` is false (`AzureRedisEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:56-61`). Both messages name the endpoints through `Describe`, which prints `(no endpoint)` for an empty list (`AzureRedisEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:70-73`).
- **Why it's built this way**: `Configure` runs inside the options pipeline, which is synchronous, while the library step is asynchronous because it acquires the first token before returning; the wait runs once per framework connection point before that connection opens, and every point shares one credential, so only the first goes over the network (`AzureRedisEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:15-20`). Registration also adds a [`HostIntegrationMarker`](group-16-aspire-orchestration.md#hostintegrationmarker) receipt the core startup tripwire checks, so setting the gate without the call fails the host instead of signing in with the access key (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:242-250`). See [ADR-134](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/134-cloud-neutral-core-vendor-adapters.html).
- **Where it's used**: registered by `AddAzureRedisEntraAuthentication` in [`AzureServiceCollectionExtensions`](#azureservicecollectionextensions) over a credential from [`AzureTokenCredentials`](#azuretokencredentials)`.Create(settings.ManagedIdentityClientId)` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:252-253`); the registration doc names the connection points it covers: the Redis client and distributed cache, the output-cache store, the SignalR backplane and the PING health check's connection (`AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:211-212`). Exercised by `MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure.Tests/Redis/AzureRedisEntraAuthenticationTests.cs`.

### CloudSqlAccessTokenSource
> MMCA.Common.Gcp · `MMCA.Common.Gcp.CloudSql` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/CloudSql/CloudSqlAccessTokenSource.cs:12` · Level 0 · class (internal sealed)

- **What it is**: the default token source behind [`CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator`](#cloudsqliamauthenticationconfigurator): it returns an OAuth2 access token for the Cloud SQL login scope from Application Default Credentials (`CloudSqlAccessTokenSource.cs:5-10`).
- **Depends on**: nothing first-party; Google `GoogleCredential` and `ITokenAccess` (`Google.Apis.Auth.OAuth2`).
- **Concept**: cross-reference [`CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator`](#cloudsqliamauthenticationconfigurator), which introduces the GCP form of token-as-password database authentication (the AWS form is [`RdsIamAuthenticationConfigurator`](#rdsiamauthenticationconfigurator)).
- **Walkthrough**: `Scope` is `https://www.googleapis.com/auth/sqlservice.login` (`CloudSqlAccessTokenSource.cs:15`). `_credential` (`CloudSqlAccessTokenSource.cs:17`) caches the scoped credential. `GetTokenAsync(cancellationToken)` (`CloudSqlAccessTokenSource.cs:22`) reads the cached field, and when it is null loads `GoogleCredential.GetApplicationDefaultAsync` and calls `CreateScoped(Scope)` (`CloudSqlAccessTokenSource.cs:27-32`), then returns `GetAccessTokenForRequestAsync(authUri: null, ...)` (`CloudSqlAccessTokenSource.cs:35`).
- **Why it's built this way**: the credential is loaded on the first request rather than at registration, so a host builds its container without Google credentials and only the first database connection needs them (`CloudSqlAccessTokenSource.cs:7-9`). The credential caches and refreshes its own token, so each call is an in-memory read until expiry nears (`CloudSqlAccessTokenSource.cs:9-10`). The lazy load is deliberately unlocked: a race between two first callers loads the credential twice and keeps the last, and a failed load is not cached, so the next call (after Npgsql's failure interval) tries again (`CloudSqlAccessTokenSource.cs:24-26`). See [ADR-113](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/113-postgresql-as-a-first-class-engine.html).
- **Where it's used**: the public constructor of [`CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator`](#cloudsqliamauthenticationconfigurator) binds its `GetTokenAsync` as the token delegate (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/CloudSql/CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:34-35`); referenced by `MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp.Tests/CloudSql/CloudSqlIamAuthenticationTests.cs`.

### GcsXmlRepository
> MMCA.Common.Gcp · `MMCA.Common.Gcp.DataProtection` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/DataProtection/GcsXmlRepository.cs:31` · Level 1 · class (internal sealed)

- **What it is**: a DataProtection `IXmlRepository` that keeps the whole key ring as one Cloud Storage object, a `<repository>` document whose children are the key elements, the same shape the Azure Blob repository uses (`GcsXmlRepository.cs:12-15`).
- **Depends on**: [`GcsObjectLocation`](#gcsobjectlocation); Google `StorageClient`, `GoogleApiException`, `UploadObjectOptions`, `DownloadObjectOptions`; `System.Xml.Linq`.
- **Concept introduced, optimistic concurrency on an object store.** Writes are read-modify-write under an object generation precondition, the Cloud Storage counterpart of an ETag: the upload carries `IfGenerationMatch` set to the generation it read (0 when the object did not exist, which lets the write only create it), so two replicas adding a key at once cannot overwrite each other (`GcsXmlRepository.cs:17-22`). `[Rubric §29, Resilience & Business Continuity]` assesses whether scale-out keeps sessions valid; one shared, lost-update-free ring is what lets every replica unprotect the others' cookies and antiforgery tokens. `[Rubric §12, Performance & Scalability]` also applies: concurrent writers converge by retry rather than by a lock.
- **Walkthrough**: `MaxAttempts` is 10 (`GcsXmlRepository.cs:34`); the root element is `repository` (`GcsXmlRepository.cs:36`); `WriterSettings` emit UTF-8 without a byte order mark (`GcsXmlRepository.cs:39`). The constructor takes the client, the location and a `retryDelay` backoff unit (`GcsXmlRepository.cs:48-53`). `GetAllElements()` returns the root's children from a fresh read (`GcsXmlRepository.cs:59-60`). `StoreElement(element, friendlyName)` (`GcsXmlRepository.cs:63`) loops up to `MaxAttempts` (`GcsXmlRepository.cs:67`): it reads the document and its generation (`GcsXmlRepository.cs:69`), appends a copy of the element so the caller's element is never re-parented into a discarded document (`GcsXmlRepository.cs:71-72`), serializes to a stream (`GcsXmlRepository.cs:74-80`), and uploads with `IfGenerationMatch = generation` (`GcsXmlRepository.cs:84-88`). A 412 `PreconditionFailed` means another writer won, so it sleeps `retryDelay * attempt` (linear backoff) and re-reads the winner's ring (`GcsXmlRepository.cs:90-97`). After the last attempt it throws naming the location and the attempt count (`GcsXmlRepository.cs:101-102`). The private `Read()` (`GcsXmlRepository.cs:105`) fetches metadata, returns an empty ring at generation 0 on 404 (`GcsXmlRepository.cs:114-119`), downloads pinned to the metadata's generation (`GcsXmlRepository.cs:125-129`), and loops when that generation vanished between the two calls (`GcsXmlRepository.cs:131-134`), returning the parsed document and generation (`GcsXmlRepository.cs:138`).
- **Why it's built this way**: pinning the download to the generation the metadata reported guarantees the generation a write is conditioned on is the one whose content it extended (`GcsXmlRepository.cs:22-23`). `IXmlRepository` is synchronous and the storage client has synchronous methods, so nothing blocks on a task; DataProtection reads the ring rarely and writes only when it creates a key (`GcsXmlRepository.cs:25-28`). See [ADR-069](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/069-shared-data-protection-key-ring.html); the Azure and AWS counterparts are [`AzureDataProtectionExtensions`](#azuredataprotectionextensions) and [`AwsDataProtectionExtensions`](#awsdataprotectionextensions).
- **Where it's used**: constructed by `PersistKeysToGoogleCloudStorage` in [`GcpDataProtectionBuilderExtensions`](#gcpdataprotectionbuilderextensions) (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/DataProtection/GcpDataProtectionBuilderExtensions.cs:38`); exercised by `MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp.Tests/DataProtection/GcsXmlRepositoryTests.cs` and `MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp.Tests/GoogleCloudDataProtectionExtensionsTests.cs`.
- **Caveats / not-in-source**: the `friendlyName` parameter is not used by this implementation (`GcsXmlRepository.cs:63-102`).

### AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator
> MMCA.Common.Azure · `MMCA.Common.Azure.PostgreSql` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/PostgreSql/AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:23` · Level 2 · class (internal sealed)

- **What it is**: an [`INpgsqlDataSourceConfigurator`](group-07-persistence-ef-core.md#inpgsqldatasourceconfigurator) that puts every PostgreSQL data source on Microsoft Entra token login for Azure Database for PostgreSQL: the password becomes an Entra access token for the `ossrdbms-aad` scope, handed to Npgsql through a periodic password provider (`AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:9-15`).
- **Depends on**: [`INpgsqlDataSourceConfigurator`](group-07-persistence-ef-core.md#inpgsqldatasourceconfigurator), [`ConnectionStringSettings`](group-07-persistence-ef-core.md#connectionstringsettings), [`DataSourcesSettings`](group-07-persistence-ef-core.md#datasourcessettings), [`AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationSettings`](#azurepostgresqlentraauthenticationsettings), [`AzurePostgreSqlAccessTokenSource`](#azurepostgresqlaccesstokensource); externals `Azure.Identity.DefaultAzureCredential` and Npgsql `NpgsqlDataSourceBuilder.UsePeriodicPasswordProvider`.
- **Concept**: token-as-password database auth, introduced on the AWS sibling [`RdsIamAuthenticationConfigurator`](#rdsiamauthenticationconfigurator) and mirrored for GCP by [`CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator`](#cloudsqliamauthenticationconfigurator); the Azure difference is that the token comes from `DefaultAzureCredential`. `[Rubric §11, Security]` assesses credential handling: no database password exists, and a connection string that does not require TLS is rejected because the token is a bearer credential sent as the password (`AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:13-15`). `[Rubric §14, Testability]` assesses substitutable dependencies; an internal constructor takes the token source as a delegate.
- **Walkthrough**: `TokenRefreshInterval` is four minutes (`AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:26`) and `FailureRetryInterval` 30 seconds (`AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:29`). The public constructor chains to `new AzurePostgreSqlAccessTokenSource(new DefaultAzureCredential()).GetTokenAsync` (`AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:34-35`); the internal one accepts any `Func<CancellationToken, Task<string>>` for tests (`AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:41-42`). `Configure(builder)` (`AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:46`) checks TLS on the builder's connection string, naming its host (`AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:50-52`), then installs `UsePeriodicPasswordProvider(GetPasswordAsync, TokenRefreshInterval, FailureRetryInterval)` (`AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:54`). `EnsureConfiguredConnectionStringsAreEncrypted(configuration)` (`AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:65`) collects the top-level `ConnectionStrings:PostgreSQLConnectionString` and every `DataSources:{name}:PostgreSQLConnectionString` (`AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:69-74`) and checks each non-blank one parsed through `NpgsqlDataSourceBuilder`, the same parser `Configure` sees (`AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:76-80`). `GetPasswordAsync` (`AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:87`) honours cancellation and awaits the token source (`AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:89-92`). `EnsureEncrypted` (`AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:95`) accepts `Require`, `VerifyCA` or `VerifyFull` (`AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:97`) and otherwise throws a message quoting `AzurePostgreSql:EntraAuthentication:Enabled`, the source and the fix (`AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:102-105`).
- **Why it's built this way**: the token lives 5 to 60 minutes, but the refresh interval is four minutes because the bound is the credential's cache: Azure.Identity hands out its cached token until fewer than five minutes remain, while Npgsql reuses what it was handed for the whole interval, so an interval under five minutes keeps every held token valid and each refresh is an in-memory read (`AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:16-22`). Checking configured connection strings at registration makes a cleartext misconfiguration fail at startup rather than on the first query (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:193-195`), while `Configure` still catches one supplied later ([ADR-070](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/070-fail-fast-configuration-contract.html)). Registration also adds a [`HostIntegrationMarker`](group-16-aspire-orchestration.md#hostintegrationmarker) receipt the core startup tripwire checks, so setting the gate without the call fails the host (`AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:197-205`). See [ADR-113](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/113-postgresql-as-a-first-class-engine.html) and [ADR-134](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/134-cloud-neutral-core-vendor-adapters.html).
- **Where it's used**: registered by `AddAzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthentication` in [`AzureServiceCollectionExtensions`](#azureservicecollectionextensions) (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:195,207`); exercised by `MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure.Tests/PostgreSql/AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthenticationTests.cs`.
- **Caveats / not-in-source**: SQL Server, SQLite and Cosmos sources are untouched; the registration doc records that SQL Server takes Entra through the SqlClient extension, and that a connection string also carrying a static `Password` is refused by Npgsql when the data source is built (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:170-173`). The database-side mapping of the Entra principal to a PostgreSQL role is provisioning, Not determinable from source.

### CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator
> MMCA.Common.Gcp · `MMCA.Common.Gcp.CloudSql` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/CloudSql/CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:23` · Level 2 · class (internal sealed)

- **What it is**: an [`INpgsqlDataSourceConfigurator`](group-07-persistence-ef-core.md#inpgsqldatasourceconfigurator) that puts every PostgreSQL data source on Cloud SQL IAM database authentication: the password becomes an OAuth2 access token for the `sqlservice.login` scope, handed to Npgsql through a periodic password provider (`CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:8-13`).
- **Depends on**: [`INpgsqlDataSourceConfigurator`](group-07-persistence-ef-core.md#inpgsqldatasourceconfigurator), [`ConnectionStringSettings`](group-07-persistence-ef-core.md#connectionstringsettings), [`DataSourcesSettings`](group-07-persistence-ef-core.md#datasourcessettings), [`CloudSqlIamAuthenticationSettings`](#cloudsqliamauthenticationsettings), [`CloudSqlAccessTokenSource`](#cloudsqlaccesstokensource); Npgsql `NpgsqlDataSourceBuilder.UsePeriodicPasswordProvider`.
- **Concept**: token-as-password database auth, introduced on the AWS sibling [`RdsIamAuthenticationConfigurator`](#rdsiamauthenticationconfigurator); the GCP difference is that the token is fetched from Google credentials rather than signed locally per host. `[Rubric §11, Security]` assesses credential handling: no database password exists, and because the token is a bearer credential sent as the password, a connection string that does not require TLS is rejected (`CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:11-13`). `[Rubric §14, Testability]` assesses whether external dependencies can be substituted; an internal constructor takes the token source as a delegate.
- **Walkthrough**: `TokenRefreshInterval` is one minute (`CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:26`) and `FailureRetryInterval` 30 seconds (`CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:29`). The public constructor chains to `new CloudSqlAccessTokenSource().GetTokenAsync` (`CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:34-35`); the internal one accepts any `Func<CancellationToken, Task<string>>` for tests (`CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:41-42`). `Configure(builder)` (`CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:46`) checks TLS on the builder's connection string, naming its host (`CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:50-52`), then installs `UsePeriodicPasswordProvider(GetPasswordAsync, TokenRefreshInterval, FailureRetryInterval)` (`CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:54`). `EnsureConfiguredConnectionStringsAreEncrypted(configuration)` (`CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:65`) collects the top-level `ConnectionStrings:PostgreSQLConnectionString` and every `DataSources:{name}:PostgreSQLConnectionString` (`CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:69-74`) and checks each non-blank one parsed through `NpgsqlDataSourceBuilder`, the same parser `Configure` sees (`CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:76-81`). `GetPasswordAsync` (`CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:90`) honours cancellation and awaits the token source (`CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:93-95`); the token does not depend on the connection settings. `EnsureEncrypted` (`CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:98`) accepts `Require`, `VerifyCA` or `VerifyFull` (`CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:100`) and otherwise throws a message quoting `CloudSql:IamAuthentication:Enabled`, the source and the fix (`CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:105-108`).
- **Why it's built this way**: the token lives an hour, but the refresh interval is one minute because the bound is the credential's cache: Google's credential serves its cached token until fewer than 225 seconds remain and blocks for a new one only under 60, while Npgsql reuses what it was handed for the whole interval; a one-minute interval keeps every held token valid, and each refresh is an in-memory read (`CloudSqlIamAuthenticationConfigurator.cs:15-21`). Checking configured connection strings at registration makes a cleartext misconfiguration fail at startup rather than on the first query (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/GcpServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:101-103`), while `Configure` still catches one supplied later ([ADR-070](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/070-fail-fast-configuration-contract.html)). See [ADR-061](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/061-runtime-secret-management.html), [ADR-113](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/113-postgresql-as-a-first-class-engine.html) and [ADR-134](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/134-cloud-neutral-core-vendor-adapters.html).
- **Where it's used**: registered by `AddCloudSqlIamAuthentication` in [`GcpServiceCollectionExtensions`](#gcpservicecollectionextensions) (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/GcpServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:103,116`); exercised by `MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp.Tests/CloudSql/CloudSqlIamAuthenticationTests.cs`.
- **Caveats / not-in-source**: SQL Server, SQLite and Cosmos sources are untouched; the registration doc comment records that Cloud SQL for SQL Server has no IAM database authentication and that behind the Cloud SQL Auth Proxy its `--auto-iam-authn` flag is used instead (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/GcpServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:79-84`). The required roles (`roles/cloudsql.instanceUser`, `roles/cloudsql.client`, same comment, line 79) are not checked by code.

### GcpDataProtectionBuilderExtensions
> MMCA.Common.Gcp · `MMCA.Common.Gcp.DataProtection` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/DataProtection/GcpDataProtectionBuilderExtensions.cs:18` · Level 2 · class (internal static)

- **What it is**: the two `IDataProtectionBuilder` extensions that wire the GCP key ring: `PersistKeysToGoogleCloudStorage` (repository) and `ProtectKeysWithGoogleCloudKms` (encryptor).
- **Depends on**: [`GcsXmlRepository`](#gcsxmlrepository), [`GcsObjectLocation`](#gcsobjectlocation), [`GcpKmsXmlEncryptor`](#gcpkmsxmlencryptor), [`GcsStorageClientFactory`](#gcsstorageclientfactory), [`GcsFileStorageSettings`](#gcsfilestoragesettings); externals `KeyManagementOptions`, `ConfigureOptions<T>`, Google `StorageClient`, `KeyManagementServiceClient`, `CryptoKeyName`.
- **Concept**: options-based plumbing of a DataProtection repository and encryptor. Each method registers an `IConfigureOptions<KeyManagementOptions>` whose factory resolves services lazily, so the client is chosen at options time rather than at registration. `[Rubric §1, SOLID]` assesses dependency inversion; here both collaborators are taken from the container when the host provided them.
- **Walkthrough**: `RetryDelay` is 50 ms, the backoff unit handed to the repository (`GcpDataProtectionBuilderExtensions.cs:21`). `PersistKeysToGoogleCloudStorage(location)` (`GcpDataProtectionBuilderExtensions.cs:34`) sets `options.XmlRepository` to a new [`GcsXmlRepository`](#gcsxmlrepository) over the host's registered `StorageClient`, or else `GcsStorageClientFactory.Create(new GcsFileStorageSettings())` (`GcpDataProtectionBuilderExtensions.cs:36-41`). `ProtectKeysWithGoogleCloudKms(keyName)` (`GcpDataProtectionBuilderExtensions.cs:54`) `TryAddSingleton`s a default `KeyManagementServiceClient.Create()` (`GcpDataProtectionBuilderExtensions.cs:56`) and sets `options.XmlEncryptor` to a [`GcpKmsXmlEncryptor`](#gcpkmsxmlencryptor) over the container's client (`GcpDataProtectionBuilderExtensions.cs:57-59`).
- **Why it's built this way**: the fallback storage client is deliberately NOT added to the container, because a default registered here would win the `TryAdd` in a later `AddGoogleCloudStorage` and silently ignore its `FileStorage:GcsServiceUrl` (`GcpDataProtectionBuilderExtensions.cs:24-29`). The KMS client, by contrast, must be in the container, because DataProtection activates [`GcpKmsXmlDecryptor`](#gcpkmsxmldecryptor) per key and that type resolves the client there (`GcpDataProtectionBuilderExtensions.cs:47-49`). See [ADR-069](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/069-shared-data-protection-key-ring.html).
- **Where it's used**: called only from [`GoogleCloudDataProtectionExtensions`](#googleclouddataprotectionextensions) (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/GoogleCloudDataProtectionExtensions.cs:110,114`), which calls the KMS step only when a Cloud KMS key name was configured and parsed (`GoogleCloudDataProtectionExtensions.cs:93-99,112-115`).

### AzureBlobFileStorageService

> MMCA.Common.Azure · `MMCA.Common.Azure.Storage` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/Storage/AzureBlobFileStorageService.cs:20` · Level 4 · class (public sealed)

- **What it is**: the Azure Blob Storage implementation of [IFileStorageService](group-07-persistence-ef-core.md#ifilestorageservice). It uploads and deletes blobs inside the one container the host configured, and returns a [Result](group-01-result-error-handling.md#result) rather than letting a storage or transport exception escape (`AzureBlobFileStorageService.cs:10-20`).
- **Depends on**: `Azure.Storage.Blobs.BlobContainerClient` and `ILogger<T>` as its two primary-constructor parameters (`AzureBlobFileStorageService.cs:20-22`), [IFileStorageService](group-07-persistence-ef-core.md#ifilestorageservice) as the contract, [FileUploadOptions](group-07-persistence-ef-core.md#fileuploadoptions) for the extra headers, and [Result](group-01-result-error-handling.md#result) / [Error](group-01-result-error-handling.md#error) for its return shapes.
- **Concept introduced, taking the container as a dependency rather than creating it.** `[Rubric §11, Security]` assesses whether an application holds only the permissions it needs, and `[Rubric §17, DevOps]` assesses whether resource provisioning lives with infrastructure rather than in application startup. The class doc is explicit (`AzureBlobFileStorageService.cs:11-13`): the container, and its public-access level, is provisioned by infrastructure and not created here. A `CreateIfNotExists` in application code would need container-management rights at runtime. `[Rubric §13, Observability & Operability]` also applies: failures are logged with the exception and translated into a domain-shaped failure whose message says nothing about Azure, so the operator sees the cause and the caller sees a stable error code.
- **Walkthrough**
